There is also a useful pure-state reduction, analogous to the relation between the WE criterion for qubit magic and stabilizer Rényi entropy.
For a fixed-parity pure fermionic state
\psi=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
, denote its correlation matrix in Eq. (
81
) as
\Gamma(\psi)
.
The
first-order fermionic antiflatness
(FAF) is defined as
99

\mathfrak{F}_{1}(\psi)=n-\frac{1}{2}\Tr\left(\Gamma(\psi)^{\mathrm{T}}\Gamma(\psi)\right).

(92)

This quantity is a faithful pure-state fermionic non-Gaussianity measure:
\mathfrak{F}_{1}(\psi)\geq 0
, with equality if and only if
\ket{\psi}
is Gaussian
99
.
In the pure-state regime analyzed below, the WE
2
-norm directly relates to FAF for small system sizes.
Thus, the same mixed-state WE construction recovers a standard pure-state fermionic non-Gaussianity test as a special case (see the proof in Appendix
F.2
).

Corollary 4

(WE
2
-criterion with rank-
1
projector is faithful for pure states of small size)
.

Let
\psi=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
be a physical pure fermionic state of
4\leq n\leq 7
qubits,
\norm{\psi}_{P_{n},\mathrm{M}_{n},2}^{2}
in Eq. (
90
) is a linear function of the first-order FAF
\mathfrak{F}_{1}(\psi)
in Eq. (
92
), explicitly,

\displaystyle\norm{\psi}_{P_{4},\mathrm{M}_{4},2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\dfrac{8+\mathfrak{F}_{1}(\psi)}{640},\qquad

\displaystyle\norm{\psi}_{P_{5},\mathrm{M}_{5},2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{40+\mathfrak{F}_{1}(\psi)}{11520},

(93)

\displaystyle\norm{\psi}_{P_{6},\mathrm{M}_{6},2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\dfrac{52+\mathfrak{F}_{1}(\psi)}{59136},\qquad

\displaystyle\norm{\psi}_{P_{7},\mathrm{M}_{7},2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{364+\mathfrak{F}_{1}(\psi)}{1537536}.

Thus for any
n
-qubit pure physical fermionic state
\psi
with
4\leq n\leq 7
,
\norm{\psi}_{P_{n},\mathrm{M}_{n},2}>C_{P_{n},\mathrm{M}_{n},2}\Longleftrightarrow\psi\notin\mathcal{G}_{n}
.

Although both constructions in Corollary
3
and in Theorem
7
use a pure resource state as the seed for WE, they are conceptually different.
The difference comes from the distinct mathematical structures of the Clifford group and the matchgate group.
In the qubit-magic case, the fourth-order Clifford-twirled response of a pure projector seed depends on the seed only through the single scalar quantity
\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi)
.
As a result, one can specify the criterion by choosing an admissible value of
\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi)
, without needing to write down the seed state explicitly.
In the fermionic case, however, the second-order matchgate twirl generally involves several Majorana sectors with different degrees once
n\geq 8
.
These additional terms are not related to a single scalar quantity analogous to
\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi)
, but depend on several correlated properties of the seed state.
Therefore, unlike the qubit magic criterion in Corollary
3
, the WE criterion for fermionic non-Gaussianity generally requires an explicit construction of the seed state
P_{n}
.
This is why we use the GHZ-based family in Eq. (
88
), whose structure allows the required WE norms and convex Gaussian thresholds to be evaluated analytically.

It is worth noting that the criteria in Theorem
7
do not guarantee to remain nontrivial for any
n
. Numerical calculation validates that WE
2,3
-criteria successfully detect non-Gaussian states
P_{n}
for
n\leq 10
, but both fail when
n=11
(see Appendix
F.2
).

VII.3
WE 2-criterion with an even-degree Majorana product

The discussion above shows that the projector seed already gives mixed-state criteria, but these criteria are not guaranteed to remain nontrivial for arbitrary system sizes.
We now show that a much simpler family of seed operators, namely Pauli strings of even Majorana degree, can already lead to nontrivial WE criteria for fermionic non-Gaussianity. Remarkably, the WE
2
-criterion with a degree-
(2\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor)
Majorana product remains nontrivial for any
n
and detects all
n
-qubit pure fermionic non-Gaussian states.

Following the discussion in Section
II.4
, the seed operator need not be positive semi-definite when
\alpha
is an even integer.
In particular, for the WE
2
-norm, the relevant functional is an
L^{2}
-norm of linear expectation values and is therefore convex.
Hence, its maximum over
\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
is attained on pure Gaussian states.
For
1\leq\ell\leq n
, define the Hermitian Majorana product

\widehat{\gamma}_{T_{\ell}}=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}Z_{j},\qquad T_{\ell}=[2\ell].

(94)

Equivalently,
\widehat{\gamma}_{T_{\ell}}
is a Majorana product of degree
2\ell
.

The resulting WE
2
-criterion is as follows; see Appendix
F.3
for the proof.

Theorem 8

(WE
2
-criterion with an even-degree Majorana product for fermionic non-Gaussianity)
.

For an
n
-qubit fermionic state
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{F}
and any
1\leq\ell\leq n
, the WE
2
-norm in criterion (

⋆

‣
II.2
) with an even-degree Majorana product
\widehat{\gamma}_{T_{\ell}}
in Eq. (
94
) and Gaussian unitaries
\mathrm{M}_{n}
in Eq. (
78
) is given by

\norm{\rho}_{\widehat{\gamma}_{T_{\ell}},\mathrm{M}_{n},2}^{2}=\binom{2n}{2\ell}^{-1}B_{\ell}(\rho),

(95)

where
B_{\ell}(\rho)
, defined in Eq. (
75
), is the degree-
2\ell
Majorana sector purity of
\rho
.
The exact convex Gaussian threshold is

C_{\widehat{\gamma}_{T_{\ell}},\mathrm{M}_{n},2}^{2}=\frac{\binom{n}{\ell}}{\binom{2n}{2\ell}}.

(96)

Consequently,

B_{\ell}(\rho)>\binom{n}{\ell}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\rho\notin\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{G}_{n}).

(97)

The contrast with Proposition
2
highlights the importance of choosing an appropriate seed operator in the WE framework.
There, the seed is a standard witness constructed from an odd-degree Majorana product, and the resulting second-order criterion only tests whether a state is parity preserving.
Thus, once restricted to physical fermionic states, it does not give a genuine test of fermionic non-Gaussianity.
By contrast, the even-degree Pauli string in Theorem
8
yields a nontrivial mixed-state criterion, whose value is simply the Majorana sector purity
B_{\ell}(\rho)
normalized by the number of degree-
2\ell
Majorana products.

The detection capability of this criterion depends strongly on the choice of
\ell
.
This dependence is already visible on pure states.
For
\ell=1
, Eq. (
95
) gives

\norm{\psi}_{\widehat{\gamma}_{T_{1}},\mathrm{M}_{n},2}^{2}=\frac{B_{1}(\psi)}{\binom{2n}{2}}=\frac{n-\mathfrak{F}_{1}(\psi)}{\binom{2n}{2}},

(98)

where
\mathfrak{F}_{1}
is the first-order FAF in Eq. (
92
).
Since
\mathfrak{F}_{1}(\psi)\geq 0
for all pure physical fermionic states and vanishes exactly on pure Gaussian states, the
\ell=1
criterion is negatively correlated with fermionic non-Gaussianity and cannot detect any pure non-Gaussian state.
Increasing
\ell
makes the criterion sensitive to higher-degree Majorana correlations.
The most useful choice turns out to be the central sector,

\ell_{*}=\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor.

(99)

For this choice, the corresponding Hermitian Majorana product is

\widehat{\gamma}_{T_{\ell_{*}}}=\prod_{j=1}^{\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor}Z_{j},\qquad T_{\ell_{*}}=\begin{cases}[n],&n\ \text{is even},\\
[n-1],&n\ \text{is odd}.\end{cases}

(100)

Remarkably, the WE criterion associated with this central Majorana sector is faithful on all fixed-parity pure states.
Therefore, it remains nontrivial for an arbitrary number of qubits.

Corollary 5

(WE
2
-criterion with central sector Majorana product is faithful for all pure fermionic states)
.

Let
\ell_{*}=\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor
.
The WE
2
-criterion with
\widehat{\gamma}_{T_{\ell_{*}}}
in Eq. (
100
) and Gaussian unitaries
\mathrm{M}_{n}
in Eq. (
78
) detects all pure fermionic non-Gaussian states, i.e., for every pure physical fermionic state
\psi=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}\in\mathcal{D}_{F}
,

\norm{\psi}_{\widehat{\gamma}_{T_{\ell_{*}}},\mathrm{M}_{n},2}>C_{\widehat{\gamma}_{T_{\ell_{*}}},\mathrm{M}_{n},2}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\psi\notin\mathcal{G}_{n}.

(101)

Equivalently, the central Majorana sector purity satisfies

B_{\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor}(\psi)\geq\binom{n}{\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor},

(102)

with equality if and only if
\psi\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
.

This corollary shows that the WE
2
-criterion with a degree-
(2\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor)
Majorana product overcomes the main limitations of the three criteria discussed above while retaining their useful features.
Compared with Proposition
2
, it gives a nontrivial test of fermionic non-Gaussianity rather than merely checking the parity-preserving condition.
Compared with the projector-based criteria in Theorem
7
, it has a much simpler mathematical form and remains nontrivial for arbitrary numbers of qubits.
Compared with FAF, it is not restricted to pure states, but provides a genuine mixed-state criterion for detecting states outside the convex hull of pure fermionic Gaussian states.
To the best of our knowledge, this is the first fermionic non-Gaussianity detection criterion for arbitrary system size with all these properties, further demonstrating the power of the WE framework for constructing resource detection protocols.

VIII
Summary and outlook

In this work, we introduced the witness expansion framework as a general method for turning a linear resource witness into a family of nonlinear, multi-copy resource detection criteria.
Starting from only a linear seed witness
W
and a set of free unitaries
\mathcal{G}
, the framework generates an
\alpha
-copy criterion whose detection capability can be tuned by
\alpha
and whose value can be analytically computed from the corresponding twirling formula.
The construction is largely independent of the detailed geometry of the free state set: the free states enter mainly through the threshold
C_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}
, which in many symmetric cases can be evaluated on a representative pure free state.
This minimal input makes the framework broadly applicable to different resource theories.
We demonstrated this universality through coherence, entanglement, qudit and qubit magic, and fermionic non-Gaussianity, recovering several known quantities and obtaining new mixed-state criteria within the same formalism.
In all these examples, a single WE criterion can detect all pure resource states, showing that the nonlinear expansion can go beyond the detection capability of the original linear witness while retaining analytical tractability.
We also showed that the detection capability of WE can be further improved by optimizing the seed witness and by choosing suitable free unitary subgroups.

A first open direction concerns the experimental accessibility of the WE criteria.
In this work, we have mainly focused on constructing functions for resource detection, rather than on designing concrete measurement protocols.
Although the polynomial quantities produced by WE can in principle be represented as observables on multiple copies of the state, this does not automatically imply low measurement complexity.
The actual cost depends on the specific resource theory and on the structure of the resulting polynomial.
For instance, some entanglement-related quantities can be measured efficiently, such as the purity and partial-transpose moment, while nonlinear magic quantities such as stabilizer Rényi entropies generally require more sophisticated protocols
51
.
It would therefore be important to analyze the measurement complexity of the magic and fermionic non-Gaussianity criteria derived using the WE framework.

Another direction is about how to further enhance the detection capability of WE.
Although our discussion of entanglement mainly served to show that WE can reproduce several known entanglement detection structures, it also reveals two important directions for improvement.
First, WE need not be restricted to unitary orbits.
The essential requirement is that the expansion maps a valid witness to another valid witness.
For entanglement, this points to possible extensions based on local filters
K_{A}\otimes K_{B}
instead of local unitaries.
Such generalized expansion families could produce richer nonlinear criteria and may strengthen the large-
\alpha
limit toward the full PPT criterion, rather than the restricted version obtained in Theorem
3
.
Second, the same example shows that symmetry averaging, while analytically useful, can also weaken detection capability by producing criteria with small coefficients in front of the relevant nonlinear invariants.
The comparison between the WE
2
-criterion and the optimal purity-based entanglement criterion indicates that these coefficients may have substantial room for improvement.
This suggests a complementary strategy: use WE as a principled starting point to identify useful nonlinear invariants, and then optimize their coefficients for a given resource theory, such as magic and fermionic non-Gaussianity.
Such an optimized criterion could provide stronger mixed-state detection and better robustness.

Finally, it would be interesting to extend WE beyond the finite-dimensional, information-theoretic setting considered in this work.
One possible direction is to develop WE for continuous-variable systems, where the relevant twirling operations may be ill-defined or analytically intractable
65
.
This may require either an appropriate continuous-variable formulation of the framework or a controlled finite-dimensional truncation before applying WE.
Another related direction is to formulate resource detection within the emerging framework of computational quantum resource theories.
Recent findings show that information-theoretic resource measures, such as certain entanglement monotones, may lose their operational meaning once the allowed computational resources are required to be efficient
72
.
It is therefore important to design detection protocols and identify resource monotones that remain efficiently computable, and to distinguish them from quantities that are formally well defined but computationally intractable.

Contents of Appendices

Appendix A
Preliminaries of the Witness expansion framework

In this appendix, we prove some basic properties of the framework for constructing nonlinear criteria and relate the violation of the WE criterion to the free robustness of a state.

A.1
Framework for constructing nonlinear criteria

We first review the notations and definitions.
For a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and density operators
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
, denote the set of free states as
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
and the set of free unitaries as
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{F}}
. Assume we have a probabilistic witness ensemble consisting of a measurable space
\Omega
equipped with a probability measure
\mu
and a family of witnesses
\left\{W_{m}\right\}_{m\in\Omega}
, with
\Tr(W_m\sigma)\geq 0,\forall\sigma\in\mathcal{F}
and
\Tr(W_m\rho)<0
for some
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})\setminus\mathcal{F}
. For each witness
W_{m}
, denote its standardized version as
\widetilde{W}_{m}=\mathbb{I}-W_{m}/\norm{W_m}_{\infty}
, such that
\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)\geq 0
for all states
\rho
and
\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\sigma)\leq 1
when
\sigma
is a free state. Denote
\widetilde{W}_{\Omega}=\{\widetilde{W}_{m}\}_{m\in\Omega}
and define the function
f_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\rho}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,

f_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\rho}(m)=\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho).

(103)

For any
1\leq\alpha<\infty
, noticing that each
\widetilde{W}_{m}\geq 0
, we denote the
L^{\alpha}(\mu)
-norm of
f_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\rho}
as

\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}=\left(\int_{\Omega}\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\mu(m)\right)^{1/\alpha}.

(104)

When
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
, the limiting value is defined as the essential supremum

\displaystyle\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{esssup}_{m\in\Omega}\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)

(105)

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{t\in\mathbb{R}\middle\mid\mu\left(\left\{m\mid\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)>t\right\}\right)=0\right\}.

For each
\alpha\in[1,\infty]
, we denote the threshold value of
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\sigma}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}
achievable by any free state
\sigma
as

C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}=\sup_{\sigma\in\mathcal{F}}\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\sigma}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}.

(106)

Denote the set of states with values beyond this threshold as

D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}\coloneqq\left\{\rho\middle\mid\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}>C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}\right\}.

(107)

Proposition 3

(Basic properties of the probabilistic witness ensemble)
.

For a probabilistic witness ensemble
\left\{\widetilde{W}_{m}\right\}
equipped with
\mu
introduced above, the following statements hold:

1.

For each
\alpha\in[1,\infty]
,

D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}\cap\mathcal{F}=\emptyset,\qquad 0\leq C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}\leq 1.

(108)

2.

For each fixed state
\rho
, the map
\alpha\mapsto\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}
is nondecreasing on
[1,\infty)
and

\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}\uparrow\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\qquad(\alpha\to\infty).

(109)

3.

The thresholds satisfy

C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}\uparrow C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}\qquad(\alpha\to\infty).

(110)

Proof of Proposition
3
.

From the definition Eq. (
104
), we have
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}\geq 0
for all states and all
\alpha<\infty
, while
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\sigma}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}\leq 1
for every free state
\sigma\in\mathcal{F}
. So
0\leq C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}\leq 1
and by definition of
C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}
in Eq. (
106
), the validity of the criterion is proven.
For
1\leq\alpha<\beta<\infty
, the monotonicity of
L^{\alpha}(\mu)
norms on a probability space yields

\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}\leq\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\beta}(\mu)}\leq\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}.

(111)

Hence
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}
is
nondecreasing and bounded above by
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}
. Let
s=\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}
and fix
\varepsilon>0
. By the definition of essential supremum,

A_{\varepsilon}=\left\{m\in\Omega\mid\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)>s-\varepsilon\right\}

(112)

has positive measure. Therefore

\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}\geq\left(\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\mu(m)\right)^{1/\alpha}\geq\mu(A_{\varepsilon})^{1/\alpha}(s-\varepsilon).

(113)

Letting
\alpha\to\infty
yields

\liminf_{\alpha\to\infty}\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}\geq s-\varepsilon.

(114)

Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, Eq. (
109
) follows.
Finally, since
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\sigma}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}
is nondecreasing in
\alpha
for each fixed
\sigma
, the sequence
C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}
is nondecreasing and bounded above by
C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}
. Moreover,

\sup_{\alpha<\infty}C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}=\sup_{\alpha<\infty}\sup_{\sigma\in\mathcal{F}}\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\sigma}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}=\sup_{\sigma\in\mathcal{F}}\sup_{\alpha<\infty}\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\sigma}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}=\sup_{\sigma\in\mathcal{F}}\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\sigma}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}=C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty},

(115)

which proves Eq. (
110
).
∎

Although the detection ability of the
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}>C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}
criterion is not monotonic with
\alpha
in general, we relate the states that are detectable by each single witness
W_{m}
(denoted as
D_{m}=\{\rho\mid\Tr(W_m\rho)<0\}
) to those detectable in the infinite-
\alpha
case. For a general probabilistic witness ensemble, the literal union of the single-witness detectable sets is not suitable to compare with the
L^{\infty}(\mu)
criterion, since the latter is governed by the essential supremum and is therefore insensitive to
\mu
-null subsets of witnesses. We instead consider

D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\mathrm{ess}}=\left\{\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})\mid\mu\left(\{m\in\Omega\mid\rho\in D_{m}\}\right)>0\right\},

(116)

that is, the set of states detected by a positive-measure subset of the ensemble. The following proposition identifies its relation to
D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}
.

Proposition 4

(Essential single-witness detection)
.

For a general probabilistic witness ensemble one has

D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\mathrm{ess}}\subseteq D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}.

(117)

If, in addition,
C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}=1
, then

D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\mathrm{ess}}=D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}.

(118)

Proof of Proposition
4
.

Let
\rho\in D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\mathrm{ess}}
. Then

\mu\left(\left\{m\mid\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)>1\right\}\right)>0.

(119)

Since

\left\{m\mid\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)>1\right\}=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\left\{m\mid\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)>1+1/n\right\},

(120)

there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
such that

\mu\left(\left\{m\mid\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)>1+1/n\right\}\right)>0.

(121)

Hence

\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}=\operatorname*{esssup}_{m}\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)\geq 1+1/n>1\geq C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty},

(122)

so we have
\rho\in D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}
.

Assume now that
C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}=1
and let
\rho\in D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}
. Then
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}>1
. For any
a
such that
1<a<\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}
, if
\mu\left(\left\{m\mid\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)>a\right\}\right)=0
, then
a
would be an essential upper bound for
\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)
, contradicting the definition of
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}
. Thus

0<\mu\left(\left\{m\mid\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)>a\right\}\right)\leq\mu\left(\left\{m\mid\Tr(\widetilde{W}_m\rho)>1\right\}\right),

(123)

which implies
\rho\in D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\mathrm{ess}}
.
∎

Furthermore, if a resource state
\rho
is detectable by the infinite-
\alpha
criterion, it must also be detected by the criterion of some finite
\alpha
and all sufficiently large
\alpha
.

Corollary 6

(Eventual detection of every state in
D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}
)
.

If
\rho\in D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}
, then there exists
\alpha_{0}(\rho)<\infty
such that

\rho\in D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha},\qquad\forall\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}(\rho).

(124)

Proof of Corollary
6
.

Choose
\delta>0
such that
3\delta<\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}-C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}
.
By Proposition
3
, for all sufficiently large
\alpha
, we have

\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}>\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}-\delta
and

C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}<C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}+\delta
,
hence
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu)}>C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\alpha}
.
∎

As a special case, the WE criterion uses the unitary twirling over a compact subgroup
\mathcal{G}
of all free unitaries. We show that the
\alpha\to\infty
criterion covers the ordinary union of single-witness detection regions, not merely the essential union. We first prove two useful lemmas.

Lemma 1

(Full support of Haar measure)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a compact subgroup of the free unitaries
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{F}}
. Every nonempty open subset of
\mathcal{G}
has strictly positive
\mu
-measures.

Proof of Lemma
1
.

Let
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
be nonempty and open, and choose
U_{0}\in\mathcal{O}
. For every
V\in\mathcal{G}
, the left translate
VU_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{O}
is an open neighborhood of
V
. Thus, the family of translates of
\mathcal{O}
covers
\mathcal{G}
. Since
\mathcal{G}
is compact, finitely many such translates suffice:

\mathcal{G}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}V_{j}U_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{O}.

(125)

If
\mu(\mathcal{O})=0
, then left-invariance of the Haar measure gives
\mu(V_{j}U_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{O})=0
for all
j
, and renders a contradiction

1=\mu(\mathcal{G})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mu(V_{j}U_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{O})=0.

(126)

So we must have
\mu(\mathcal{O})>0
.
∎

Lemma 2

(Essential supremum equals supremum)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a compact subgroup of the free unitaries
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{F}}
. For every fixed state
\rho
, the map
U\mapsto\Tr(U^\dagger\widetilde{W}U\rho)
is continuous on
\mathcal{G}
, and therefore

\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}=\operatorname*{esssup}_{U\in\mathcal{G}}\Tr(U^\dagger\widetilde{W}U\rho)=\sup_{U\in\mathcal{G}}\Tr(U^\dagger\widetilde{W}U\rho).

(127)

Proof of Lemma
2
.

Continuity of
U\mapsto\Tr(U^\dagger\widetilde{W}U\rho)
follows from continuity of multiplication and adjoint in a finite-dimensional system. Assume this function attains its maximum at
U_{\rho}
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
and define

\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}=\left\{U\in\mathcal{G}\middle\mid\Tr(U^\dagger\widetilde{W}U\rho)>\Tr(U_\rho^\dagger\widetilde{W}U_\rho\rho)-\varepsilon\right\},

(128)

it is a nonempty open neighborhood of
U_{\rho}
, hence
\mu(\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon})>0
by Lemma
1
. Therefore

\operatorname*{esssup}_{U\in\mathcal{G}}\Tr(U^\dagger\widetilde{W}U\rho)\geq\Tr(U_\rho^\dagger\widetilde{W}U_\rho\rho)-\varepsilon.

(129)

Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary and
\operatorname*{esssup}\leq\sup
always holds, Eq. (
127
) follows.
∎

Now we prove that the detectable states by the WE infinite-
\alpha
criterion actually cover all the states that could be detected by any witness constructed by
W
under free unitary rotation.

Proposition 5

(Recovery of the ordinary single-witness union under twirling)
.

For each
U\in\mathcal{G}
, denote the set of states that could be detected by witness
U^{\dagger}WU
as
D_{U}
, then

\bigcup_{U\in\mathcal{G}}D_{U}\subseteq D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}.

(130)

If, in addition,
C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}=1
, then

\bigcup_{U\in\mathcal{G}}D_{U}=D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}.

(131)

Proof of Proposition
5
.

Let
\rho\in\bigcup_{U\in\mathcal{G}}D_{U}
, it is detected by some witness
U_{0}^{\dagger}WU_{0}
, by Lemma
2
,

\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}=\sup_{U\in\mathcal{G}}\Tr(U^\dagger\widetilde{W}U\rho)\geq\Tr(U_0^\dagger\widetilde{W}U_0\rho)>1.

(132)

On the other hand,
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\sigma}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\leq 1
for every
\sigma\in\mathcal{F}
, hence
C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}\leq 1
and we have
\rho\in D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}
.

If
C_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}=1
and
\rho\in D_{\widetilde{W}_{\Omega},\mu,\infty}
, then
\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}>1
. By Lemma
2
there exists
U_{0}\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\Tr(U_0^\dagger\widetilde{W}U_0\rho)=\norm{f_{\widetilde{W}_\Omega,\rho}}_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}>1
, which means
\rho\in D_{U_{0}}\subseteq\bigcup_{U\in\mathcal{G}}D_{U}
.
∎

A.2
Proof of Proposition
1

In this appendix, we prove that the amount by which the WE
\alpha
-criterion is violated gives a lower bound on the free robustness of the state.
Recall that the free robustness of
\rho
with respect to the free set
\mathcal{F}
is defined as

\mathcal{R}(\rho)=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\middle\mid\exists\tau\in\mathcal{F}\text{ s.t. }\frac{\rho+t\tau}{1+t}\in\mathcal{F}\right\}.

(133)

This quantity measures the minimum amount of any free state that must be mixed with
\rho
so that the resulting state becomes free. We then prove Proposition
1
in the main text, which provides a lower bound of
\mathcal{R}(\rho)
using the WE
\alpha
-norm
\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}
and the threshold value
C_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}
for free states.

Proof of Proposition
1
.

Let
t\geq 0
be any feasible value in the definition of
\mathcal{R}(\rho)
.
Then there exists a free state
\tau\in\mathcal{F}
such that

\sigma=\frac{\rho+t\tau}{1+t}\in\mathcal{F}.

(134)

Equivalently,

\rho=(1+t)\sigma-t\tau.

(135)

For any positive semi-definite operator
\widetilde{W}
and any compact subgroup
\mathcal{G}
of free unitaries, we define the corresponding expectation function for state
\rho
w.r.t. the family of positive semi-definite operators
\mathcal{G}^{\dagger}\widetilde{W}\mathcal{G}=\left\{U^{\dagger}\widetilde{W}U\middle\mid U\in\mathcal{G}\right\}
as (cf. Eq. (
2
) in the main text)

f_{\mathcal{G}^{\dagger}\widetilde{W}\mathcal{G},\rho}(U)=\Tr(U^\dagger\widetilde W U\rho).

(136)

By linearity in the state, we have

f_{\mathcal{G}^{\dagger}\widetilde{W}\mathcal{G},\rho}=(1+t)f_{\mathcal{G}^{\dagger}\widetilde{W}\mathcal{G},\sigma}-tf_{\mathcal{G}^{\dagger}\widetilde{W}\mathcal{G},\tau}.

(137)

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, the WE
\alpha
-norm of
\rho
is defined as the
L^{\alpha}(\mu_{\mathcal{G}})
-norm of
f_{\mathcal{G}^{\dagger}\widetilde{W}\mathcal{G},\rho}
(cf. Eq. (
9
) in the main text), Minkowski’s inequality gives

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}=\norm{f_{\mathcal{G}^\dagger\widetilde{W}\mathcal{G},\rho}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu_{\mathcal{G}})}\leq(1+t)\norm{f_{\mathcal{G}^\dagger\widetilde{W}\mathcal{G},\sigma}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu_{\mathcal{G}})}+t\norm{f_{\mathcal{G}^\dagger\widetilde{W}\mathcal{G},\tau}}_{L^{\alpha}(\mu_{\mathcal{G}})}.

(138)

We have,

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}\leq(1+t)\norm{\sigma}_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}+t\norm{\tau}_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}\leq(1+2t)C_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha},

(139)

since both
\sigma
and
\tau
are free states, their WE
\alpha
-norms are bounded by the free state threshold.
This simplifies to

t\geq\frac{\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}-C_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}}{2C_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}},

(140)

for every feasible
t
.
Taking the infimum over all feasible
t
in the definition of
\mathcal{R}(\rho)
gives

\mathcal{R}(\rho)\geq\frac{\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}-C_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}}{2C_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}}.

(141)

This finishes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Coherence

Fix the reference basis
\{\ket{j}\}_{j=0}^{d-1}
of a
d
-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with
d\geq 2
. The set of free states is

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{I}=\left\{\sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})\middle|\bra{j}\sigma\ket{k}=0\ \textup{for all }j\neq k\right\},

(142)

and we choose free unitaries as the diagonal unitaries

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d)=\left\{\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}e^{i\theta_{k}}\ket{k}\bra{k}\middle|\theta_{k}\in[0,2\pi)\right\}.

(143)

We parametrize a generic free unitary by

U_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}e^{i\theta_{k}}\ket{k}\bra{k},\qquad\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{0},\cdots,\theta_{d-1})\in[0,2\pi)^{d},

(144)

and we write
\mu_{\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}}
for the normalized Haar measure on
\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}
, namely

\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}}}[f(U)]=\int_{[0,2\pi)^{d}}f(U_{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\frac{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\theta}}{(2\pi)^{d}}.

(145)

We choose the witness and its standardized version as

W=\Xi=\frac{1}{d-1}\sum_{j\neq k}\ket{k}\bra{j},\qquad\widetilde{W}=\widetilde{\Xi}=\mathbb{I}-\Xi.

(146)

For every
\sigma\in\mathcal{F}
we have
\Tr(\Xi\sigma)=0
, while for the coherent state

\rho_{-}=\frac{(\ket{0}-\ket{1})(\bra{0}-\bra{1})}{2}

(147)

we get

\Tr(\Xi\rho_-)=-\frac{1}{d-1}<0,

(148)

so
\Xi
is a valid coherence witness.

Denote the random off-diagonal witness expectation as

\xi(U_{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\rho)=(d-1)\Tr(U_{\boldsymbol\theta}^\dagger\Xi U_{\boldsymbol\theta}\rho)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k=0\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\bra{j}\rho\ket{k}e^{i(\theta_{j}-\theta_{k})},

(149)

and the corresponding standardized witness expectation is

\Tr(U_{\boldsymbol\theta}^\dagger\widetilde{\Xi}U_{\boldsymbol\theta}\rho)=1-\frac{\xi(U_{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\rho)}{d-1}.

(150)

In this way, we have

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\Xi},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d),\alpha}^{\alpha}=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}}}\left[\left(1-\frac{\xi(U_{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\rho)}{d-1}\right)^{\alpha}\right].

(151)

We now derive the closed form of
\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\Xi},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d),\alpha}
in terms of
\rho
for an arbitrary
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. We first introduce a useful definition.

Definition 1

(Eulerian adjacency matrices)
.

For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
be the set of all matrices
M=(M_{j,k})_{j,k=0}^{d-1}
in
\mathcal{M}_{d}(\mathbb{N})
such that

M_{j,j}=0,\forall j,\qquad\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}M_{j,k}=\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}M_{k,j},\forall j,\qquad\sum_{j,k=0}^{d-1}M_{j,k}=\ell.

(152)

Equivalently,
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
is the set of adjacency matrices of directed Eulerian multigraphs with
\ell
edges and no self-loops.

The expectation value of
\ell
-th moment of
\xi(U,\rho)
over the Haar measure on
\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d)
is given as follows.

Proposition 6

(Eulerian moment formula)
.

For any state
\rho
, let
\xi(U,\rho)
be defined as in Eq. (
149
), then for integer
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}}}\left[\xi(U,\rho)^{\ell}\right]=\ell!\sum_{M\in\mathcal{E}_{\ell}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k=0\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\frac{\bra{j}\rho\ket{k}^{M_{j,k}}}{M_{j,k}!}.

(153)

Proof of Proposition
6
.

We use the multinomial theorem and introduce running index
M_{j,k}
to denote how many times
\bra{j}\rho\ket{k}e^{i(\theta_{j}-\theta_{k})}
appears in each term,

\xi(U_{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\rho)^{\ell}=\sum_{\sum_{j\neq k}M_{j,k}=\ell}\frac{\ell!}{\prod_{j\neq k}M_{j,k}!}\prod_{j\neq k}\left(\bra{j}\rho\ket{k}e^{i(\theta_{j}-\theta_{k})}\right)^{M_{j,k}}.

(154)

Taking the Haar average over the random diagonal unitary group
\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d)
gives

\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}}}[\xi^{\ell}]=\sum_{\sum_{j\neq k}M_{j,k}=\ell}\frac{\ell!\prod_{j\neq k}\bra{j}\rho\ket{k}^{M_{j,k}}}{\prod_{j\neq k}M_{j,k}!}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left[\exp\left(i\sum_{j\neq k}M_{j,k}(\theta_{j}-\theta_{k})\right)\right].

(155)

The exponent can be regrouped as

\sum_{j\neq k}M_{j,k}(\theta_{j}-\theta_{k})=\sum_{r=0}^{d-1}\theta_{r}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}M_{r,k}-\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}M_{j,r}\right).

(156)

Because the phases are independent and uniformly distributed, the average is nonzero if and only if each coefficient of
\theta_{r}
vanishes, namely

\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}M_{j,r}=\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}M_{r,k},\qquad\forall r\in\{0,\cdots,d-1\}.

(157)

Together with
M_{j,j}=0
and
\sum_{j,k}M_{j,k}=\ell
, this is exactly the defining condition for
M\in\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
. Hence, the sum collapses to Eq. (
153
).
∎

Together with Proposition
6
, we obtain a closed form evaluation for
\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\Xi},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d),\alpha}
for any state
\rho
and positive integer
\alpha
.
We take the binomial expansion in Eq. (
150
) inside Eq. (
151
):

\left(1-\frac{\xi(U,\rho)}{d-1}\right)^{\alpha}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\ell}\left(-\frac{1}{d-1}\right)^{\ell}\xi(U,\rho)^{\ell}.

(158)

Averaging over
U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}}
and inserting Eq. (
154
) yields

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\Xi},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d),\alpha}^{\alpha}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\ell}\left(-\frac{1}{d-1}\right)^{\ell}\ell!\sum_{M\in\mathcal{E}_{\ell}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k=0\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\frac{\bra{j}\rho\ket{k}^{M_{j,k}}}{M_{j,k}!},

(159)

which is Eq. (
20
) in the main text.

We show that the threshold value of
\norm{\sigma}_{\widetilde{\Xi},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d),\alpha}
achievable by any incoherent state
\sigma
is simply 1, which completes the statement of WE
\alpha
-criterion.

Theorem 9

(Exact incoherent threshold)
.

For any
\alpha\geq 1
and every free state
\sigma\in\mathcal{I}
, we have

\norm{\sigma}_{\widetilde{\Xi},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d),\alpha}=1.

(160)

Consequently, the exact threshold for incoherent states is

C_{\widetilde{\Xi},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d),\alpha}=1,\qquad\forall\alpha\geq 1.

(161)

Proof of Theorem
9
.

If
\sigma\in\mathcal{I}
, Eq. (
149
) gives
\xi(U,\sigma)=0,\forall U\in\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d)
, hence

\norm{\sigma}_{\widetilde{\Xi},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d),\alpha}=\left(\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}}}[1^{\alpha}]\right)^{1/\alpha}=1.

(162)

∎

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Combining the expression for WE
\alpha
-norm in Eq. (
159
) and Theorem
9
, we finish the proof of Theorem
1
.
∎

Explicitly, the WE
1
-criterion is trivial and the explicit WE
2
-criterion is provided as below.

Corollary 7

(WE
2
-criterion for coherence)
.

For any state
\rho
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}}}\left[\xi(U,\rho)^{2}\right]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k=0\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\absolutevalue{\bra{j}\rho\ket{k}}^{2}.

(163)

Consequently, the WE 2-criterion for coherence is given by

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\Xi},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d),2}^{2}=1+\frac{1}{(d-1)^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k=0\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\absolutevalue{\bra{j}\rho\ket{k}}^{2},\qquad C_{\widetilde{\Xi},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}(d),2}=1.

(164)

Proof of Corollary
7
.

For
\ell=2
, the only matrices in
\mathcal{E}_{2}
are the directed 2-cycles
j\leftrightarrow k
with
j\neq k
. Therefore Eq. (
153
) becomes

\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{U}_{\textup{diag}}}}[\xi(U,\rho)^{2}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k=0\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\bra{j}\rho\ket{k}\bra{k}\rho\ket{j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k=0\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\absolutevalue{\bra{j}\rho\ket{k}}^{2},

(165)

using Hermiticity of
\rho
. Substituting this into Eq. (
158
) and Eq. (
151
) with
\alpha=2
gives Eq. (
164
).
∎

For the resource theory of coherence, the
\alpha=2
criterion already characterizes all coherent states, showing the power of the WE framework.

Appendix C
Entanglement

C.1
Proof of Theorem
2
and Corollary
1

Let
\mathcal{H}_{A}\cong\mathcal{H}_{B}\cong\mathbb{C}^{d}
, and let
\rho
be a density operator on
\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
. The free states are those separable under the bipartition
A
and
B

\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{Sep}=\left\{\sum_{k}p_{k}\sigma_{A}^{k}\otimes\sigma_{B}^{k}\middle\mid\sum_{k}p_{k}=1,p_{k}\geq 0,\sigma_{\bullet}^{k}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}),\forall k\right\},

(166)

and the group of free unitaries are chosen as the tensor products of local unitaries on each subsystem

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d)=\left\{U_{A}\otimes U_{B}\middle\mid U_{\bullet}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\bullet})\right\}.

(167)

We choose the SWAP operator
W=\mathbb{S}=\sum_{j,k=0}^{d-1}\ket{j,k}\bra{k,j}
between the corresponding basis of subsystems
A
and
B
as the entanglement witness and set the standardized witness
\widetilde{W}=\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}=\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{S}
, since
\mathbb{S}
has eigenvalues
\pm 1
. We denote

y_{\rho}(U_{A},U_{B})=\Tr\left[\mathbb{S}(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})\rho(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})^{\dagger}\right],

(168)

then we have

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}^{\alpha}=\mathbb{E}_{U_{A},U_{B}}\left[\left(1-y_{\rho}(U_{A},U_{B})\right)^{\alpha}\right].

(169)

We first notice that the Haar average for
U_{A}\otimes U_{B}
over
\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d)
is equal to that of a relative unitary
\mathbb{I}\otimes U
.

Lemma 3

(Reduction to a relative unitary)
.

For every
\alpha>0
,

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}^{\alpha}=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d)}}\left[\Tr\left[(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{S})(\mathbb{I}\otimes U)\rho(\mathbb{I}\otimes U^{\dagger})\right]^{\alpha}\right],

(170)

where
U
is Haar distributed on
\mathrm{U}(d)
.

Proof of Lemma
3
.

Using
\mathbb{S}(V\otimes V)=(V\otimes V)\mathbb{S}
for all
V\in\mathrm{U}(d)
and cyclicity of the trace,

\displaystyle y_{\rho}(U_{A},U_{B})

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[\mathbb{S}(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})\rho(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})^{\dagger}\right]

(171)

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{I}\otimes U_{A}^{\dagger}U_{B})\rho(\mathbb{I}\otimes U_{B}^{\dagger}U_{A})\right].

Thus
y_{\rho}(U_{A},U_{B})
depends only on the relative unitary
U=U_{A}^{\dagger}U_{B}
. Since
U_{A}
and
U_{B}
are independent Haar-distributed,
U
is Haar-distributed as well. Inserting into Eq. (
169
) finishes the proof.
∎

From now on, we abbreviate
y_{\rho}(U)=\Tr\left[\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{I}\otimes U)\rho(\mathbb{I}\otimes U^{\dagger})\right]
.
When
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, by the binomial theorem

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}^{\alpha}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\ell}(-1)^{\ell}m_{\ell}(\rho),

(172)

where
m_{\ell}(\rho)=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{U}(d)}}[y_{\rho}(U)^{\ell}]
and especially
m_{0}(\rho)=1
.
Write matrix elements of
\rho
in the computational basis as
\rho_{i\mu,j\nu}
, where Latin indices refer to subsystem
A
and Greek indices to subsystem
B
. Since

\langle i,\mu|\mathbb{S}|j,\nu\rangle=\delta_{i,\nu}\delta_{\mu,j},\qquad\langle i,\mu|(\mathbb{I}\otimes U)|j,\nu\rangle=\delta_{i,j}U_{\mu\nu},

(173)

a direct expansion gives

y_{\rho}(U)=\sum_{i,j,\mu,\nu}\rho_{i\mu,j\nu}U_{j\mu}\overline{U_{i\nu}}.

(174)

Hence, for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

\displaystyle m_{\ell}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{U}(d)}}\left[y_{\rho}(U)^{\ell}\right]

(175)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{\ell}\\
j_{1},\cdots,j_{\ell}\\
\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{\ell}\\
\nu_{1},\cdots,\nu_{\ell}\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}\rho_{i_{r}\mu_{r},j_{r}\nu_{r}}\right)\int_{\mathrm{U}(d)}\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}U_{j_{r}\mu_{r}}\overline{U_{i_{r}\nu_{r}}}\mathrm{d}U.

We now use the standard Weingarten formula

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{U}(d)}\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}U_{a_{r}b_{r}}\overline{U_{a^{\prime}_{r}b^{\prime}_{r}}}\mathrm{d}U=\sum_{\pi,\omega\in S_{\ell}}\left(\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}\delta_{a_{r},a^{\prime}_{\pi(r)}}\right)\left(\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}\delta_{b_{r},b^{\prime}_{\omega(r)}}\right)\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(\pi^{-1}\omega),

(176)

where
\mathrm{Wg}_{d}
denotes the unitary Weingarten function
33
. Applying this to Eq. (
175
), relabeling
\omega\mapsto\omega^{-1}
, and using that
\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(\omega)=\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(\omega^{-1})
for
\omega\in S_{\ell}

and that
\mathrm{Wg}_{d}
is a class function on
S_{\ell}
, we obtain

m_{\ell}(\rho)=\sum_{\pi,\omega\in S_{\ell}}\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(\pi\omega)T_{\pi,\omega}(\rho),

(177)

where

T_{\pi,\omega}(\rho)=\sum_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{\ell}}\sum_{\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{\ell}}\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}\rho_{i_{r}\mu_{r},i_{\pi(r)}\mu_{\omega(r)}}.

(178)

Equivalently, if
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}^{(\ell)}
denotes the permutation operator for
\pi\in S_{\ell}
on
\ell
copies,

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}^{(\ell)}\left(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\ell}\right)=v_{\pi^{-1}(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\pi^{-1}(\ell)},

(179)

then

T_{\pi,\omega}(\rho)=\Tr\left[(\mathbb{P}_{\pi}^{(\ell),A}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(\ell),B})\rho^{\otimes\ell}\right].

(180)

Inserting Eq. (
177
) and Eq. (
180
) into Eq. (
172
), we obtain

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}^{\alpha}=1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\ell}(-1)^{\ell}\sum_{\pi,\omega\in S_{\ell}}\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(\pi\omega)\Tr\left[(\mathbb{P}_{\pi}^{(\ell),A}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(\ell),B})\rho^{\otimes\ell}\right],

(181)

which is Eq. (
25
) in the main text.

We then show the explicit calculation for
\ell=1,2,3
and prove Corollary
1
.

Proof of Corollary
1
.

Denote

\rho_{A}=\Tr_{B}(\rho),\qquad\rho_{B}=\Tr_{A}(\rho),

(182)

and define the abbreviations

\displaystyle r_{2}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\rho^2),

\displaystyle r_{3}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\rho^3),

(183)

\displaystyle r_{2,A}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\rho_A^2),

\displaystyle r_{2,B}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\rho_B^2),

\displaystyle r_{3,A}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\rho_A^3),

\displaystyle r_{3,B}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\rho_B^3),

\displaystyle u_{A}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[\rho^{2}(\rho_{A}\otimes\mathbb{I})\right],

\displaystyle u_{B}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[\rho^{2}(\mathbb{I}\otimes\rho_{B})\right],

\displaystyle\tau_{3}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[(\rho^{\mathrm{T}_{B}})^{3}\right],

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[\rho(\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B})\right].

•

For
\ell=1
, the Weingarten value

\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(e)=\frac{1}{d},

(184)

so
m_{1}(\rho)=1/d
.

•

For
\ell=2
, the symmetric group
S_{2}=\{e,s\}
, with
s=(12)
. The Weingarten values are

\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(e)=\frac{1}{d^{2}-1},\qquad\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(s)=-\frac{1}{d(d^{2}-1)}.

(185)

By Eq. (
177
),

m_{2}(\rho)=\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(e)\left(T_{e,e}+T_{s,s}\right)+\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(s)\left(T_{e,s}+T_{s,e}\right).

(186)

The four contractions are elementary:

\displaystyle T_{e,e}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\rho)^{2}=1,

\displaystyle T_{s,s}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\rho^2)=r_{2},

(187)

\displaystyle T_{s,e}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\rho_A^2)=r_{2,A},

\displaystyle T_{e,s}

\displaystyle=\Tr(\rho_B^2)=r_{2,B}.

Substituting into Eq. (
186
) gives

m_{2}(\rho)=\frac{1+r_{2}-(r_{2,A}+r_{2,B})/d}{d^{2}-1}.

(188)

By Eq. (
172
), we have

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}^{2}=1-2m_{1}+m_{2}=1-\frac{2}{d}+\frac{1+r_{2}-(r_{2,A}+r_{2,B})/d}{d^{2}-1}.

(189)

•

For
\ell=3
, write

s_{1}=(12),\qquad s_{2}=(13),\qquad s_{3}=(23),\qquad c_{+}=(123),\qquad c_{-}=(132).

(190)

The Weingarten values on the three conjugacy classes of
S_{3}
are, for
d\geq 3
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(e)

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{2}-2}{d(d^{2}-1)(d^{2}-4)},

(191)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(s_{i})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(d^{2}-1)(d^{2}-4)},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Wg}_{d}(c_{\pm})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{d(d^{2}-1)(d^{2}-4)}.

We list the contractions
T_{\pi,\omega}(\rho)
needed to evaluate Eq. (
177
)
(cf. Eq (
183
)):

\displaystyle T_{e,e}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle T_{s_{i},e}

\displaystyle=r_{2,A},

\displaystyle T_{e,s_{i}}

\displaystyle=r_{2,B},

\displaystyle T_{s_{i},s_{i}}

\displaystyle=r_{2},

(192)

\displaystyle T_{c_{\pm},e}

\displaystyle=r_{3,A},

\displaystyle T_{e,c_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=r_{3,B},

\displaystyle T_{c_{\pm},c_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=r_{3},

\displaystyle T_{c_{+},c_{-}}

\displaystyle=T_{c_{-},c_{+}}=\tau_{3},

\displaystyle T_{s_{i},s_{j}}

\displaystyle=v,(i\neq j),

\displaystyle T_{c_{\pm},s_{i}}

\displaystyle=u_{A},

\displaystyle T_{s_{i},c_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=u_{B}.

All identities follow from direct contractions of tensor indices. We provide as example one representative computation for each orbit under relabeling of the three copies.

First,

T_{s_{1},e}=\Tr\left[\left(\mathbb{P}_{(12)}^{(3),A}\otimes\mathbb{I}\right)\rho^{\otimes 3}\right]=\Tr(\rho_A^2)\Tr(\rho)=r_{2,A}.

(193)

Similarly,
T_{e,s_{1}}=r_{2,B}
,
T_{s_{1},s_{1}}=r_{2}
,
T_{c_{+},e}=r_{3,A}
,
T_{e,c_{+}}=r_{3,B}
, and
T_{c_{+},c_{+}}=r_{3}
.

Next,

\displaystyle T_{c_{+},c_{-}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\\
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}\end{subarray}}\rho_{i_{1}\mu_{1},i_{2}\mu_{3}}\rho_{i_{2}\mu_{2},i_{3}\mu_{1}}\rho_{i_{3}\mu_{3},i_{1}\mu_{2}}

(194)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\\
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}\end{subarray}}(\rho^{\mathrm{T}_{B}})_{i_{1}\mu_{3},i_{2}\mu_{1}}(\rho^{\mathrm{T}_{B}})_{i_{2}\mu_{1},i_{3}\mu_{2}}(\rho^{\mathrm{T}_{B}})_{i_{3}\mu_{2},i_{1}\mu_{3}}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[(\rho^{\mathrm{T}_{B}})^{3}\right]=\tau_{3}.

By symmetry,
T_{c_{-},c_{+}}=\tau_{3}
as well.

For a pair of distinct transpositions, say
(s_{1},s_{2})
,

\displaystyle T_{s_{1},s_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\\
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}\end{subarray}}\rho_{i_{1}\mu_{1},i_{2}\mu_{3}}\rho_{i_{2}\mu_{2},i_{1}\mu_{2}}\rho_{i_{3}\mu_{3},i_{3}\mu_{1}}

(195)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{3}}\rho_{i_{1}\mu_{1},i_{2}\mu_{3}}(\rho_{A})_{i_{2},i_{1}}(\rho_{B})_{\mu_{3},\mu_{1}}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[\rho(\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B})\right]=v.

All other pairs
(s_{i},s_{j})
with
i\neq j
are equivalent by relabeling tensor copies.
For a transposition and a
3
-cycle, choose
(c_{+},s_{1})
, to get

\displaystyle T_{c_{+},s_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\\
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}\end{subarray}}\rho_{i_{1}\mu_{1},i_{2}\mu_{2}}\rho_{i_{2}\mu_{2},i_{3}\mu_{1}}\rho_{i_{3}\mu_{3},i_{1}\mu_{3}}

(196)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},i_{3},\mu_{1}}(\rho^{2})_{i_{1}\mu_{1},i_{3}\mu_{1}}(\rho_{A})_{i_{3},i_{1}}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[\rho^{2}(\rho_{A}\otimes\mathbb{I})\right]=u_{A}.

Likewise,

\displaystyle T_{s_{1},c_{+}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\\
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}\end{subarray}}\rho_{i_{1}\mu_{1},i_{2}\mu_{2}}\rho_{i_{2}\mu_{2},i_{1}\mu_{3}}\rho_{i_{3}\mu_{3},i_{3}\mu_{1}}

(197)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},\mu_{1},\mu_{3}}(\rho^{2})_{i_{1}\mu_{1},i_{1}\mu_{3}}(\rho_{B})_{\mu_{3},\mu_{1}}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[\rho^{2}(\mathbb{I}\otimes\rho_{B})\right]=u_{B}.

All remaining identities are obtained from these representatives by permuting the three tensor copies.

We now evaluate Eq. (
177
). Grouping the pairs
(\pi,\omega)\in S_{3}\times S_{3}

according to the cycle type of
\pi\omega
, we find

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\omega=e}T_{\pi,\omega}

\displaystyle=1+3r_{2}+2\tau_{3},

(198)

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\omega\textup{ transposition}}T_{\pi,\omega}

\displaystyle=3r_{2,A}+3r_{2,B}+6u_{A}+6u_{B},

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\omega\textup{ 3-cycle}}T_{\pi,\omega}

\displaystyle=2r_{3,A}+2r_{3,B}+6v+2r_{3}.

Therefore

\displaystyle m_{3}(\rho)={}

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}-2}{d(d^{2}-1)(d^{2}-4)}\left(1+3r_{2}+2\tau_{3}\right)

(199)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{(d^{2}-1)(d^{2}-4)}\left(3r_{2,A}+3r_{2,B}+6u_{A}+6u_{B}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{d(d^{2}-1)(d^{2}-4)}\left(2r_{3,A}+2r_{3,B}+6v+2r_{3}\right).

Finally,

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),3}^{3}=1-3m_{1}+3m_{2}-m_{3},

(200)

where
m_{1}=1/d
and
m_{2}
is given by Eq. (
188
). Substituting these values
yields Eq. (
29
).

∎

We then calculate the threshold value

C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}
for all separable states.

Theorem 10

(Exact separable threshold)
.

For every
\alpha\geq 1
,

C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}=\left(\frac{d-1}{d+\alpha-1}\right)^{1/\alpha}.

(201)

In particular,

C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),1}=\frac{d-1}{d},\qquad C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}=\sqrt{\frac{d-1}{d+1}},\qquad C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),3}=\sqrt[3]{\frac{d-1}{d+2}}.

(202)

Proof of Theorem
10
.

Notice that
\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}^{\alpha}
in Eq. (
169
) is convex in
\rho
, because for every fixed
U_{A},U_{B}
the map
\rho\mapsto 1-y_{\rho}(U_{A},U_{B})
is linear, while
x\mapsto x^{\alpha}
is convex on
[0,\infty)
and averaging preserves convexity. Therefore, its maximum is attained at an extreme point of
\mathrm{Sep}
, namely a pure product state.
Let
\sigma=|a\rangle\langle a|\otimes|b\rangle\langle b|
. Then (cf. Eq. (
171
))

y_{\sigma}(U_{A},U_{B})=\absolutevalue{\langle a|U_A^\dagger U_B|b\rangle}^{2}.

(203)

If
U
is Haar distributed on
\mathrm{U}(d)
, the random variable

\zeta=|\langle a|U|b\rangle|^{2}

(204)

has the Beta
(1,d-1)
distribution, with density
(d-1)(1-z)^{d-2}
for
z\in[0,1]
. Hence

\displaystyle\norm{\sigma}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{U}(d)}}[(1-\zeta)^{\alpha}]

(205)

\displaystyle=(d-1)\int_{0}^{1}(1-z)^{\alpha+d-2}dz

\displaystyle=\frac{d-1}{d+\alpha-1},

and

C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}=\left(\frac{d-1}{d+\alpha-1}\right)^{1/\alpha}.

(206)

∎

Proof of Theorem
2
.

Combining the expression for WE
\alpha
-norm in Eq. (
181
) and Theorem
10
, we finish the proof of Theorem
2
.
∎

C.2
Optimal choice for second-order criterion

We prove that when
\alpha=2
, among all
W_{2}
restricted to the form of Eq. (
16
), taking
\widetilde{W}=\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}
yields the best detection capability.

Theorem 11

(Optimal seed operator of the WE
2
-criterion for entanglement)
.

Let
O
be any Hermitian operator on
\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
. If a state
\rho
can be detected by the WE 2-criterion constructed by
O
, then the WE 2-criterion constructed by
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}=\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{S}
also detects
\rho
. Equivalently,

\norm{\rho}_{O,\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}>C_{O,\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}>C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}.

(207)

Proof of Theorem
11
.

Consider the second-order local unitary twirling of
O^{\otimes 2}
(see e.g. Corollary 13 in Ref.
82
)

\mathbb{E}_{U_{A},U_{B}\sim\mu_{\mathrm{U}(d)}}\left[{(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})^{\dagger}}^{\otimes 2}O^{\otimes 2}(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})^{\otimes 2}\right]=\alpha_{0}\mathbb{I}+\alpha_{A}\mathbb{S}_{A}+\alpha_{B}\mathbb{S}_{B}+\alpha_{A,B}\mathbb{S}_{A}\mathbb{S}_{B},

(208)

where
\mathbb{S}_{A}
(
\mathbb{S}_{B}
) denotes the SWAP operator between two copies of subsystem
A
(
B
).
The coefficients are

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\Tr O)^{2}-\Tr[(\Tr_{B}O)^{2}]/d-\Tr[(\Tr_{A}O)^{2}]/d+\Tr(O^2)/d^{2}}{(d^{2}-1)^{2}},

(209)

\displaystyle\alpha_{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr[(\Tr_{B}O)^{2}]-(\Tr O)^{2}/d-\Tr(O^2)/d+\Tr[(\Tr_{A}O)^{2}]/d^{2}}{(d^{2}-1)^{2}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr[(\Tr_{A}O)^{2}]-(\Tr O)^{2}/d-\Tr(O^2)/d+\Tr[(\Tr_{B}O)^{2}]/d^{2}}{(d^{2}-1)^{2}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{A,B}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr(O^2)-\Tr[(\Tr_{B}O)^{2}]/d-\Tr[(\Tr_{A}O)^{2}]/d+(\Tr O)^{2}/d^{2}}{(d^{2}-1)^{2}}.

Choose an orthonormal (w.r.t. Hilbert–Schmidt inner product) basis
\{P_{j}\}_{j=0}^{d^{2}-1}
with
P_{0}=\mathbb{I}/\sqrt{d}
of
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathbb{C}^{d}\right)
and expand

O=\sum_{j,k=0}^{d^{2}-1}o_{j,k}P_{j}\otimes P_{k}.

(210)

Through a direct calculation, the coefficients in Eq. (
209
) satisfy

\alpha_{A,B}=\frac{\sum_{j,k\geq 1}\absolutevalue{o_{j,k}}^{2}}{(d^{2}-1)^{2}}\geq 0,\quad\alpha_{A}+\frac{\alpha_{A,B}}{d}=\frac{\sum_{j\geq 1}\absolutevalue{o_{j,0}}^{2}}{d(d^{2}-1)}\geq 0,\quad\alpha_{B}+\frac{\alpha_{A,B}}{d}=\frac{\sum_{j\geq 1}\absolutevalue{o_{0,j}}^{2}}{d(d^{2}-1)}\geq 0.

(211)

Using Eq. (
208
), the WE 2-norm is given by (cf. Eq. (
183
))

\norm{\rho}_{O,\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}^{2}=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{A}r_{2,A}+\alpha_{B}r_{2,B}+\alpha_{A,B}r_{2},

(212)

and the corresponding threshold value

C_{O,\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}^{2}=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{A}+\alpha_{B}+\alpha_{A,B}.

(213)

We have

\norm{\rho}_{O,\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}^{2}-C_{O,\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}^{2}=\alpha_{A,B}(r_{2}-1)+\alpha_{A}(r_{2,A}-1)+\alpha_{B}(r_{2,B}-1).

(214)

Since
r_{2,A},r_{2,B},r_{2}\leq 1
, and notice that Eq. (
211
) implies

\alpha_{A}(r_{2,A}-1)\leq-\frac{\alpha_{A,B}}{d}(r_{2,A}-1),\qquad\alpha_{B}(r_{2,B}-1)\leq-\frac{\alpha_{A,B}}{d}(r_{2,B}-1).

(215)

If
\rho
can be detected by the WE 2-criterion constructed by
O
, then

\displaystyle 0<\norm{\rho}_{O,\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}^{2}-C_{O,\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{A,B}(r_{2}-1)-\frac{\alpha_{A,B}}{d}(r_{2,A}-1)-\frac{\alpha_{A,B}}{d}(r_{2,B}-1)

(216)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{A,B}}{d}\left(dr_{2}-r_{2,A}-r_{2,B}-d+2\right).

Since
\alpha_{A,B}\geq 0
, we have in this case
\alpha_{A,B}>0
, so

dr_{2}>r_{2,A}+r_{2,B}+d-2,

(217)

which is exactly the condition of WE 2-criterion constructed by
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}
, see Eq. (
32
).
∎

C.3
Proof of Theorem
3

We then take the
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
limit, the WE criterion reduces to asking whether the partial transposition
\rho^{\mathrm{T}_{B}}
has a negative value when projected to the basis of the family of maximally entangled states. We reformulate Theorem
3
as the theorem below.

Theorem 12

(WE
\infty
-criterion for entanglement)
.

Let
|\Phi_{d}\rangle:=\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}|j,j\rangle/{\sqrt{d}}
and define the maximally entangled orbit
as

\mathrm{ME}_{d}:=\left\{(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})|\Phi_{d}\rangle\middle\mid U_{A},U_{B}\in\mathrm{U}(d)\right\}.

(218)

For every state
\rho
,

\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}=1-d\min_{\ket{\Phi}\in\mathrm{ME}_{d}}\bra{\Phi}\rho^{\mathrm{T}_{B}}\ket{\Phi}.

(219)

Moreover,

\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}=1,

(220)

and more precisely,

\log C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}=-\frac{\log\left(\alpha/(d-1)\right)}{\alpha}+O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\right),\qquad\alpha\to\infty.

(221)

Consequently,

C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}=1-\frac{\log\left(\alpha/(d-1)\right)}{\alpha}+O\left(\frac{\log^{2}\alpha}{\alpha^{2}}\right).

(222)

Proof of Theorem
12
.

We first prove the
L^{\alpha}\to L^{\infty}
limit. Set

f(U_{A},U_{B})=1-y_{\rho}(U_{A},U_{B}).

(223)

The domain
\mathrm{U}(d)\times\mathrm{U}(d)
is compact, and
f
is continuous; hence
f
attains its maximum
M=\max f
. Since the Haar measure is normalized,
\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}=\norm{f}_{L^{\alpha}}
. Clearly
\norm{f}_{L^{\alpha}}\leq M
for all
\alpha
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
, and let
\left(U_{A}^{(0)},U_{B}^{(0)}\right)
be a point where
f=M
. By continuity, the open set

\Omega_{\varepsilon}=\left\{(U_{A},U_{B}):f(U_{A},U_{B})>M-\varepsilon\right\}

(224)

has strictly positive Haar measure. Therefore

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}\geq\left[(M-\varepsilon)^{\alpha}\mu(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\right]^{1/\alpha}=(M-\varepsilon)\mu(\Omega_{\varepsilon})^{1/\alpha}.

(225)

Letting
\alpha\to\infty
gives

\liminf_{\alpha\to\infty}\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}\geq M-\varepsilon.

(226)

Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, one obtains

\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}=M=1-\min_{U_{A},U_{B}}y_{\rho}(U_{A},U_{B}).

(227)

Next, we rewrite the maximum in terms of
\rho^{\mathrm{T}_{B}}
. Notice that

\mathbb{S}^{\mathrm{T}_{B}}=d|\Phi_{d}\rangle\langle\Phi_{d}|.

(228)

Using
\Tr(O_1O_2)=\Tr(O_1^{\mathrm{T}_B}O_2^{\mathrm{T}_B})
for
O_{1},O_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Tr\left[\mathbb{S}(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})\rho(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})^{\dagger}\right]

(229)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Tr\left[\mathbb{S}^{\mathrm{T}_{B}}\left((U_{A}\otimes U_{B})\rho(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})^{\dagger}\right)^{\mathrm{T}_{B}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\langle\Phi_{d}|(U_{A}\otimes U_{B}^{*})\rho^{\mathrm{T}_{B}}(U_{A}^{\dagger}\otimes U_{B}^{\mathrm{T}})|\Phi_{d}\rangle.

Define

\ket{\Phi}=(U_{A}^{\dagger}\otimes U_{B}^{\mathrm{T}})|\Phi_{d}\rangle\in\mathrm{ME}_{d}.

(230)

Then

y_{\rho}(U_{A},U_{B})=\Tr\left[\mathbb{S}(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})\rho(U_{A}\otimes U_{B})^{\dagger}\right]=d\bra{\Phi}\rho^{\mathrm{T}_{B}}\ket{\Phi}.

(231)

Taking the maximum over local unitaries is therefore equivalent to taking the minimum over
\mathrm{ME}_{d}
, and we conclude that

\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}=1-d\min_{\ket{\Phi}\in\mathrm{ME}_{d}}\bra{\Phi}\rho^{\mathrm{T}_{B}}\ket{\Phi}.

(232)

Finally, for
\alpha\geq 1
,

C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}=\left(\frac{d-1}{d+\alpha-1}\right)^{1/\alpha},

(233)

so

\displaystyle\log C_{\widetilde{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{U}_{\textup{loc}}(d,d),\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{\log(d-1)-\log(d+\alpha-1)}{\alpha}

(234)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\log(\alpha/(d-1))}{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(1+\frac{d-1}{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\log(\alpha/(d-1))}{\alpha}+O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\right).

∎

Appendix D
Qudit magic for odd prime local dimension

We denote the dimension
d=q^{n}
. In the field
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, addition and multiplication are well-defined in the sense of modulo
q
. Denote the
q
-th root of unity as
\omega_{q}=e^{i2\pi/q}
and define the Weyl operators for
z,x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
as

\begin{gathered}Z(z)\ket{t}=\omega_{q}^{z\cdot t}\ket{t},\qquad X(x)\ket{t}=\ket{t+x},\quad\textup{for basis vector }\ket{t}\in\mathcal{H}\left((\mathbb{C}_{q})^{\otimes n}\right),\\
T_{z,x}=\omega_{q}^{-2^{-1}z\cdot x}Z(z)X(x).\end{gathered}

(235)

Introducing the symplectic form for
u=(z_{u},x_{u})
and
v=(z_{v},x_{v})
in
V\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
as

[u,v]=z_{u}\cdot x_{v}-x_{u}\cdot z_{v},

(236)

the multiplication of two Weyl operators is

T_{u}T_{v}=\omega_{q}^{2^{-1}[u,v]}T_{u+v}.

(237)

Using this as the group action, the
n
-qudit Heisenberg-Weyl group with local dimension
q
is defined as

\mathsf{P}_{n,q}=\left\{\omega_{q}^{a}T_{z,x}\middle\mid a\in\mathbb{Z}_{q},z,x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\right\}.

(238)

The set of free unitaries is the
n
-qudit Clifford group, which is the normalizer of
\mathsf{P}_{n,q}

\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q}=\{U\in\mathrm{U}(d)\mid U\mathsf{P}_{n,q}U^{\dagger}=\mathsf{P}_{n,q}\}.

(239)

The set of free states (
n
-qudit stabilizer polytope) and the set of stabilizer states are

\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{STAB}_{n,q}=\textup{Conv}(\Sigma_{n,q}),\qquad\Sigma_{n,q}=\left\{U\ket{0^n}\middle|U\in\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q}\right\}.

(240)

The set of pure stabilizer states is characterized by the discrete Hudson’s theorem
45
, by evaluating the discrete Wigener function.

Lemma 4

(Discrete Hudson’s theorem
45
)
.

For an odd prime integer
q
and any
\rho\in\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{H}\left((\mathbb{C}_{q})^{\otimes n}\right)\right)
, define its discrete Wigner function evaluated at
u\in V
as

W_{\rho}(u)=\frac{1}{d}\Tr(A_u\rho),

(241)

where
A_{u}
is the phase space point operator

A_{u}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in V}\omega_{q}^{-[u,v]}T_{v}^{\dagger}.

(242)

Then an
n
-qudit pure state vector
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}\left((\mathbb{C}_{q})^{\otimes n}\right)
is a stabilizer state if and only if its discrete Wigner function takes nonnegative values at all points,

\ket{\psi}\in\Sigma_{n,q}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad W_{\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}}(u)\geq 0,\forall u\in V.

(243)

We can simply choose
W=A_{0^{2n}}
and
\widetilde{W}=\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}}=\mathbb{I}-A_{0^{2n}}
to be the witness to be expanded, since

\sigma\in\mathrm{STAB}_{n,q}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\Tr(A_{0^{2n}}\sigma)=dW_{\sigma}(0^{2n})\geq 0,

(244)

by convexity, and
A_{0^{2n}}
can detect the non-stabilizer state
\rho=(\ket{1}-\ket{-1})(\bra{1}-\bra{-1})/2
.

We first show that, given solely state moments
\left\{\Tr(\rho^k)\right\}_{k}
, regardless of how many orders there are, we cannot distinguish stabilizer mixtures from genuinely magic states.

Proposition 7

(Universality of state spectrum in stabilizer polytope)
.

For every
\rho\in\mathcal{D}((\mathbb{C}^{q})^{\otimes n})
, there exists
\sigma\in\mathrm{STAB}_{n,q}
such that

\operatorname{spec}(\sigma)=\operatorname{spec}(\rho).

(245)

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\Tr(\sigma^k)

\displaystyle=\Tr(\rho^k),

\displaystyle\qquad\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

(246)

Proof of Proposition
7
.

Let

\rho=\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{j}\ket{\psi_j}\!\bra{\psi_j}

be a spectral decomposition of
\rho
, where
\lambda_{j}\geq 0
and
\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{j}=1
. Consider the computational basis

\{\ket{j}\}_{j=0}^{d-1}=\{\ket{0,\cdots,0},\cdots,\ket{q-1,\cdots,q-1}\}

of
(\mathbb{C}^{q})^{\otimes n}
. Each computational basis vector is a pure stabilizer state. Define

\sigma=\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{j}\ket{j}\!\bra{j}
.
Since
\sigma
is a convex combination of pure stabilizer states, we have
\sigma\in\mathrm{STAB}_{n,q}
.

By construction, the eigenvalues of
\sigma
are precisely
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j=0}^{d-1}
, counted with multiplicity. Hence Eq. (
245
) holds. Since the spectral moments are symmetric functions of the eigenvalues, for every positive integer
k
one obtains

\Tr(\sigma^k)=\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{j}^{k}=\Tr(\rho^k),

(247)

which proves Eq. (
246
).
∎

The proposition implies that any magic detection criterion must depend on more than the eigenvalues of the state. Equivalently, no quantity that depends only on the collection
\left\{\Tr(\rho^k)\right\}_{k\geq 1}
can detect whether
\rho
lies outside
\mathrm{STAB}_{n,q}
. Especially if the group of free unitaries
\mathcal{G}
forms a unitary
t
-design, the first
t
-th order twirlings are the same as those of the Haar random unitaries, which, by Schul-Weyl duality, only relate to state moments. Indeed, when evaluating

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{W},\mathcal{G},\alpha}=\left(\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{G}}}\left[\Tr(\widetilde W U\rho U^\dagger)^{\alpha}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},

(248)

for
\alpha\leq t
, all information encoded by
\rho
is in the form of the
k\leq\alpha
twirling formula

\Phi_{\mathcal{G}}^{(k)}(\rho)=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{G}}}\left[U^{\otimes k}\rho^{\otimes k}{U^{\dagger}}^{\otimes k}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\textup{Haar}}}\left[U^{\otimes k}\rho^{\otimes k}{U^{\dagger}}^{\otimes k}\right],

(249)

which consists of terms related to permutation operators
33
and leads to only state moments of
\rho

\Tr(\mathbb{P}_\pi^{(k)}\rho^{\otimes k})=\prod_{c\in\textup{cycle}(\pi)}\Tr(\rho^{\abs{c}}).

(250)

Notice that in our setting, when
q
is an odd prime, the
n
-qudit Clifford group
\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q}
is a unitary 2-design
35
, but not a unitary 3-design
46
;
12
. Proposition
7
implies the WE
1,2
-criteria are both trivial, we start with
\alpha=3
and prove Theorem
4
.

Proof of Theorem
4
.

We need to calculate

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},3}^{3}=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q}}}\left[\Tr(\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}} U\rho U^\dagger)^{3}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q}}}\left[\Tr\left((\mathbb{I}-U^{\dagger}A_{0^{2n}}U)\rho\right)^{3}\right].

(251)

Since
q
is an odd prime, the Clifford group has the semidirect-product form
46

\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q}\simeq V\rtimes\mathrm{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Z}_{q}),

(252)

so each Clifford
U\in\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q}
can be written as
U=T_{a}\mu(F)
, where
\mu
is a symplectic representation of
\mathrm{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{Z}_{q})
. The action on the Weyl operator
T_{u}
is given by

\mu(F)T_{u}\mu(F)^{\dagger}=T_{Fu},\forall u\in V.

(253)

From Eq. (
242
), we have

\mu(F)A_{u}\mu(F)^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in V}\omega_{q}^{-[u,v]}\mu(F)T_{v}^{\dagger}\mu(F)^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{w\in V}\omega_{q}^{-[u,F^{-1}w]}T_{w}^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{w\in V}\omega_{q}^{-[Fu,w]}T_{w}^{\dagger}=A_{Fu},

(254)

and
UA_{u}U^{\dagger}=A_{Fu+a}
. So that
\{U^{\dagger}A_{0^{2n}}U\}_{U\in\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q}}
is uniformly distributed over
\{A_{u}\}_{u\in V}
. Then Eq. (
251
) is reduced to

\displaystyle\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},3}^{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{u\in V}\left(1-\Tr(A_u\rho)\right)^{3}=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{u\in V}\left(1-dW_{\rho}(u)\right)^{3}

(255)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{3}{d}\sum_{u\in V}W_{\rho}(u)+3\sum_{u\in V}W_{\rho}(u)^{2}-d\sum_{u\in V}W_{\rho}(u)^{3}.

A direct calculation yields for all
\rho
,

\sum_{u\in V}W_{\rho}(u)=1,\qquad\sum_{u\in V}W_{\rho}(u)^{2}=\frac{1}{d}\Tr(\rho^2),

(256)

and thus Eq. (
45
) follows:

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},3}^{3}=1-\frac{3}{d}+\frac{3}{d}\Tr(\rho^2)-d\sum_{u\in V}W_{\rho}(u)^{3}.

(257)

For any pure stabilizer state vector
\ket{\psi}
, its discrete Wigner function
W_{\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}}
takes value
1/d
on
d
phase space points, and value
0
on the remaining
d^{2}-d
points
45
, so for
\alpha\geq 1
,

\norm{\ketbra{\psi}{\psi}}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},\alpha}^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{u\in V}\left(1-dW_{\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}}(u)\right)^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{d^{2}}(d^{2}-d)=1-\frac{1}{d}=C_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},\alpha}^{\alpha}.

(258)

By Observation
1
, it is also the maximum value obtainable for all states in
\mathrm{STAB}_{n,q}
, which proves Eq. (
47
). Furthermore, when
\ket{\psi}
is a pure state, notice that
\norm{A_u}_{\infty}=1
and
W_{\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}}(u)\leq 1/d
by Eq. (
241
), we have

\norm{\ketbra{\psi}{\psi}}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},3}^{3}-C_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},3}^{3}=-d\sum_{u\in V}W_{\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}}(u)^{3}+\frac{1}{d}=\sum_{u\in V}W_{\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}}(u)^{2}\left(1-dW_{\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}}(u)\right)\geq 0.

(259)

So
\norm{\ketbra{\psi}{\psi}}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},3}=C_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},3}
if and only if
W_{\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}}(u)
only take nonnegative values
1/d
or
0
, which implies
\ket{\psi}
is a stabilizer state by the discrete Hudson’s theorem.
∎

We then prove Theorem
5
, which instead uses a subgroup
\mathcal{G}_{X}=\left\{X(x)\middle|x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\right\}
and takes the average over a column of phase-space point operators.

Proof of Theorem
5
.

We need to calculate

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathcal{G}_{X},2}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mu_{\mathcal{G}_{X}}}\left[\Tr\left((\mathbb{I}-U^{\dagger}A_{0^{2n}}U)\rho\right)^{2}\right].

(260)

Similar to the proof of Theorem
4
, we recognize
U^{\dagger}A_{0}U
for
U=X(x)\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
as a phase-space point operator

X(x)^{\dagger}A_{0^{2n}}X(x)=A_{0^{n},-x}.

(261)

Then we have

\displaystyle\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathcal{G}_{X},2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}\left(1-\Tr(A_{0,-x}\rho)\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}\left(1-dW_{\rho}(0^{n},x)\right)^{2}

(262)

\displaystyle=1-2\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}W_{\rho}(0^{n},x)+d\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}W_{\rho}(0^{n},x)^{2}.

Notice that
A_{u}
has largest eigenvalue
1
, so for
\sigma\in\mathrm{STAB}_{n,q}
,
0\leq W_{\sigma}(u)\leq 1/d
, we have

\norm{\sigma}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathcal{G}_{X},2}^{2}\leq 1-\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}W_{\sigma}(0^{n},x)\leq 1.

(263)

The upper bound is attainable. Take a pure stabilizer state whose Wigner support is the affine Lagrangian subspace

(\iota,0^{n})+L_{X},\qquad L_{X}=\left\{(0^{n},x)\mid x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\right\},

(264)

where
\iota=(1,0,\cdots,0)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. This support is disjoint from the slice
\left\{(0^{n},x)\mid x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\right\}
, so all the slice Wigner values vanish and the value of the functional is exactly
1
.
The threshold value for all stabilizer states is

C_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathcal{G}_{X},2}^{2}=\sup_{\sigma\in\mathrm{STAB}_{n,q}}\norm{\sigma}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathcal{G}_{X},2}^{2}=1,

(265)

For any
n
and odd prime
q
, we now provide a state
\rho_{2}
that can be detected by the second-order criterion in Theorem
5
but cannot by the third-order criterion in Theorem
4
, and another pure state vector
\ket{\psi_3}
on the contrary.

•

Consider

\rho_{2}=\frac{4\mathbb{I}-3A_{0^{2n}}}{4d-3}.

(266)

It is a valid density matrix with eigenvalues
\{1/(4d-3),7/(4d-3)\}
and has trace
1
. Using
\Tr(A_uA_v)=d\delta_{u,v}
, the discrete Wigner function of
\rho_{2}
at
u\in V
takes the value

W_{\rho_{2}}(u)=\frac{4-3d\delta_{u,0^{2n}}}{d(4d-3)}=\begin{cases}\frac{4-3d}{d(4d-3)},\qquad&u=0^{2n},\\
\frac{4}{d(4d-3)},&u\neq 0^{2n}.\end{cases}

(267)

Then a direct calculation yields for
d\geq 3
,

\displaystyle\norm{\rho_2}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathcal{G}_{X},2}^{2}-C_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathcal{G}_{X},2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{d-2}{(4d-3)^{2}}>0,

(268)

\displaystyle\norm{\rho_2}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},3}^{3}-C_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},3}^{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(d-1)(128d^{2}-487d+414)}{d(4d-3)^{3}}<0.

•

Consider

\ket{\psi_3}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{0^n}+\ket{\iota}\right),

(269)

where
\iota
is the short notation for the string
(1,0,\cdots,0)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. Using
A_{(z,x)}\ket{y}=\omega_{q}^{2z\cdot(x-y)}\ket{-y+2x}
, its discrete Wigner function evaluates as

W_{\outerproduct{\psi_3}{\psi_3}}(z,x)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{2d},&x=0^{n},\\
\dfrac{1}{d}\cos(\frac{2\pi z_1}q),&x=\frac{\iota}{2},\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(270)

where
z_{1}
is the first digit of
z
.
By Eq. (
259
),
\ket{\psi_3}
is a non-stabilizer state vector
since

\norm{\ketbra{\psi_3}{\psi_3}}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},3}^{3}-C_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathrm{Cl}_{n,q},3}^{3}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{2d},&q=3,\\
\dfrac{3}{4d},&q>3.\end{cases}

(271)

And it is direct to verify

\norm{\ketbra{\psi_3}{\psi_3}}_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathcal{G}_{X},2}^{2}-C_{\widetilde{A}_{0^{2n}},\mathcal{G}_{X},2}^{2}=-\frac{5}{2d}<0.

(272)

∎

Appendix E
Qubit magic

E.1
Proof of Theorem
6

In
n
-qubit magic state resource theory, the set of free states (denoted as the stabilizer polytope
\mathrm{STAB}_{n}
) is the convex hull of all
n
-qubit pure stabilizer states, i.e., those that can be generated from
\ket{0^n}
using a Clifford unitary,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{STAB}_{n}=\textup{Conv}(\Sigma_{n}),\quad\textup{with }\Sigma_{n}=\left\{U\ket{0^n}\middle\mid U\in\mathrm{Cl}_{n}\right\}.

(273)

Here, the
n
-qubit Clifford group
\mathrm{Cl}_{n}
is the normalizer of
n
-qubit Pauli group
\mathsf{P}_{n}
, which is also the set of free unitaries,

\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{Cl}_{n}=\{U\in\mathrm{U}(d)\mid U\mathsf{P}_{n}U^{\dagger}=\mathsf{P}_{n}\}.

(274)

Let the conjugacy classes of
S_{4}
be indexed by cycle type

\mu\in\{[1,1,1,1],[2,1,1],[2,2],[3,1],[4]\},

(275)

with class sizes

\displaystyle m_{[1,1,1,1]}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle m_{[2,1,1]}

\displaystyle=6,

\displaystyle m_{[2,2]}

\displaystyle=3,

\displaystyle m_{[3,1]}

\displaystyle=8,

\displaystyle m_{[4]}

\displaystyle=6.

(276)

For an operator
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
, define

p_{\lambda}(A)=\Tr(\mathbb{P}_\pi^{(4)}A^{\otimes 4}),

(277)

where
\pi\in S_{4}
has the cycle type
\lambda
and the permutation operator
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}^{(4)}
is defined in Eq. (
179
).
For an arbitrary state
\rho
, write the state moments

r_{k}=\Tr(\rho^k),\qquad k=2,3,4,

(278)

then

\displaystyle p_{[1,1,1,1]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle p_{[2,1,1]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=r_{2},

\displaystyle p_{[2,2]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=r_{2}^{2},

\displaystyle p_{[3,1]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=r_{3},

\displaystyle p_{[4]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=r_{4}.

(279)

Denote the set of
n
-qubit phase-free Pauli operators as

\mathcal{P}_{n}=\left\{\mathbb{I},X,Y,Z\right\}^{\otimes n}.

(280)

We introduce the projector that appears as a commutant of the fourfold tensor of Cliffords
118
;
12

Q=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}P^{\otimes 4},

(281)

and its orthogonal complement projector
Q^{\perp}=\mathbb{I}-Q
.
Define

q_{\lambda}(A)=\Tr(\mathbb{P}_\pi^{(4)}QA^{\otimes 4}),

(282)

for any
\pi\in S_{4}
with the cycle type
\lambda
, and in particular

\displaystyle q_{[1,1,1,1]}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}[\Tr(A P)]^{4},

(283)

\displaystyle q_{[2,1,1]}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\Tr((A P)^2)[\Tr(A P)]^{2},

\displaystyle q_{[2,2]}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}[\Tr((A P)^2)]^{2},

\displaystyle q_{[3,1]}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\Tr((A P)^3)\Tr(A P),

\displaystyle q_{[4]}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\Tr((A P)^4).

We recall the Schur-Weyl decomposition (see, e.g., Theorem S.1 in Ref.
96
)

(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes 4}\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\vdash 4:\ell(\lambda)\leq d}W_{\lambda}\otimes S_{\lambda},

(284)

where
W_{\lambda}
and
S_{\lambda}
are the Weyl module and the Specht module, respectively. Using the same notation as in Ref.
118
, we denote for each partition
\lambda\vdash 4
,
QP_{\lambda}
projects onto
W_{\lambda}^{+}\otimes S_{\lambda}
and
Q^{\perp}P_{\lambda}
projects onto
W_{\lambda}^{-}\otimes S_{\lambda}
. Define

d_{\lambda}=\dim S_{\lambda},\qquad D_{\lambda}^{\pm}=\dim W_{\lambda}^{\pm}.

(285)

Their values are listed in Table
1
(copied from Table 1 in Ref.
118
).

Dimensions of Specht and irreducible components, which appear in the fourth-order Clifford twirling formula.

Denote the orthogonal projector to the submodule
W_{\lambda}\otimes S_{\lambda}
as (cf. Eq. (

))

P_{\lambda}=\frac{d_{\lambda}}{24}\sum_{\omega\in S_{4}}\chi_{\lambda}(\omega)\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)},

(286)

we have

\sum_{\lambda\vdash 4:\ell(\lambda)\leq d}P_{\lambda}=\mathbb{I}.

(287)

Here, the character value
\chi_{\lambda}(\omega)
is determined by the cycle type
\mu
of
\omega
, rewritten as
\chi_{\lambda}(\mu)
, and can be calculated using the Murnaghan-Nakayama rule, listed in Table

.

.

We further define the character transforms (cf. Eq. (
276
), Table
2
, Eq. (
279
) and Eq. (
283
))

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d_{\lambda}}\Tr(P_\lambda A^{\otimes 4})=\frac{1}{24}\sum_{\mu}m_{\mu}\chi_{\lambda}(\mu)p_{\mu}(A),

(288)

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d_{\lambda}}\Tr(P_\lambda QA^{\otimes 4})=\frac{1}{24}\sum_{\mu}m_{\mu}\chi_{\lambda}(\mu)q_{\mu}(A),

Explicitly,

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{[4]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+6r_{2}+3r_{2}^{2}+8r_{3}+6r_{4}}{24},

(289)

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{[3,1]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+2r_{2}-r_{2}^{2}-2r_{4}}{8},

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{[2,2]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+3r_{2}^{2}-4r_{3}}{12},

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{[2,1,1]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-2r_{2}-r_{2}^{2}+2r_{4}}{8},

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{[1,1,1,1]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-6r_{2}+3r_{2}^{2}+8r_{3}-6r_{4}}{24}.

Similarly, omitting the
\rho
in
q_{\lambda}(\rho)
for simplicity,

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{[4]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{[1,1,1,1]}+6q_{[2,1,1]}+3q_{[2,2]}+8q_{[3,1]}+6q_{[4]}}{24},

(290)

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{[3,1]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{[1,1,1,1]}+2q_{[2,1,1]}-q_{[2,2]}-2q_{[4]}}{8},

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{[2,2]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{[1,1,1,1]}+3q_{[2,2]}-4q_{[3,1]}}{12},

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{[2,1,1]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{[1,1,1,1]}-2q_{[2,1,1]}-q_{[2,2]}+2q_{[4]}}{8},

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{[1,1,1,1]}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{[1,1,1,1]}-6q_{[2,1,1]}+3q_{[2,2]}+8q_{[3,1]}-6q_{[4]}}{24}.

We now calculate the WE
4
-norm with a positive semi-definite operator
O
as seed and reformulate Theorem
6
in the main text as the theroem below.

Theorem 13

(WE
4
-norm for qubit magic)
.

For every
n
-qubit state
\rho
, the WE
4
-norm in criterion (

⋆

‣
II.2
) with a positive semi-definite operator
O
and
n
-qubit Clifford group
\mathrm{Cl}_{n}
in Eq. (
274
) is given by

\displaystyle\norm{\rho}_{O,\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}^{4}=\sum_{\lambda\vdash 4:\ell(\lambda)\leq d}d_{\lambda}\left[\frac{\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\rho)\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(O)}{D_{\lambda}^{+}}+\frac{\left(\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(\rho)-\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\rho)\right)\left(\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(O)-\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(O)\right)}{D_{\lambda}^{-}}\right],

(291)

where the values of
d_{\lambda}
,
D_{\lambda}^{\pm}
are given in Tabel
1
and
\widehat{p}_{\lambda}
,
\widehat{q}_{\lambda}
are defined in Eq. (
288
). The coefficients in Theorem
6
are
