a_{\lambda}=\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(O),\qquad b_{\lambda}=\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(O)-\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(O).

(292)

From Eq. (
248
), we have

\norm{\rho}_{O,\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}^{4}=\Tr\left[O^{\otimes 4}\Phi_{\mathrm{Cl}_{n}}^{(4)}(\rho^{\otimes 4})\right].

(293)

We start from the fourth-order Clifford twirling formula for
A\in\mathcal{L}\left(\left(\mathbb{C}^{d}\right)^{\otimes 4}\right)
(see Theorem S.5 in Ref.
96
),

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathrm{Cl}_{n}}^{(4)}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24}\sum_{\lambda\vdash 4:\ell(\lambda)\leq d}d_{\lambda}\sum_{\omega\in S_{4}}\left[\frac{1}{D_{\lambda}^{+}}\Tr\left(AQ\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right)Q+\frac{1}{D_{\lambda}^{-}}\Tr\left(AQ^{\perp}\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right)Q^{\perp}\right]\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}P_{\lambda},

(294)

where
Q^{\perp}=\mathbb{I}-Q
is defined as in Eq. (
281
) and
P_{\lambda}
is defined in Eq. (
286
).
We first prove a useful lemma, which can simplify the calculation of Eq. (
294
).

Lemma 5

(Simplification for permutation-invariant operator)
.

For any operator
B
that commutes with all permutations, i.e., satisfies

[B,\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}]=0,\qquad\forall\omega\in S_{4},

(295)

for the term in Eq. (
294
), we have

\frac{d_{\lambda}}{24}\sum_{\omega\in S_{4}}\Tr\left(B\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right)\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}P_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{d_{\lambda}}\Tr(BP_\lambda)P_{\lambda},

(296)

where
P_{\lambda}
is defined in Eq. (
286
).

Proof of Lemma
5
.

By the Schur-Weyl duality, the natural permutation representation of the symmetric group
S_{4}
on
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes 4}
,

\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{d}:\omega\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}

(297)

is decomposed as

\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{d}(\omega)=\bigoplus_{\mu\vdash 4:\ell(\mu)\leq d}\mathbb{I}_{W_{\mu}}\otimes\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{\mu}(\omega),\qquad\omega\in S_{4},

(298)

where
\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{\mu}:S_{4}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(S_{\mu})
is the irreducible representation of
\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{d}
on the Specht module
S_{\mu}
.

Notice that
[B,P_{\mu}]=0
.
Hence
B
preserves each Schur-Weyl block and can be decomposed as

B=B\mathbb{I}=B\left(\sum_{\mu\vdash 4:\ell(\mu)\leq d}P_{\mu}\right)=B\left(\sum_{\mu\vdash 4:\ell(\mu)\leq d}P_{\mu}^{2}\right)=\sum_{\mu\vdash 4:\ell(\mu)\leq d}P_{\mu}BP_{\mu}.

(299)

Fix the partition
\mu
, on the block
W_{\mu}\otimes S_{\mu}
, write

P_{\mu}BP_{\mu}=\sum_{a,b}|a\rangle\langle b|\otimes M_{ab},

(300)

where
\{|a\rangle\}
is a basis of
W_{\mu}
and each
M_{ab}\in\textup{End}(S_{\mu})
.
Because
P_{\mu}BP_{\mu}
still commutes with every permutation, we have

(\mathbb{I}_{W_{\mu}}\otimes\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{\mu}(\omega))(P_{\mu}BP_{\mu})=(P_{\mu}BP_{\mu})(\mathbb{I}_{W_{\mu}}\otimes\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{\mu}(\omega)),\qquad\forall\omega\in S_{4}.

(301)

Comparing coefficients of
|a\rangle\langle b|
yields

\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{\mu}(\omega)M_{ab}=M_{ab}\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{\mu}(\omega),\qquad\forall\omega\in S_{4}.

(302)

Since
S_{\mu}
is an irreducible
S_{4}
-module, Schur’s lemma implies that each
M_{ab}
must be a scalar multiple of the identity on
S_{\mu}
:

M_{ab}=c_{ab}\mathbb{I}_{S_{\mu}}.

(303)

Therefore

P_{\mu}BP_{\mu}=\sum_{a,b}c_{ab}|a\rangle\langle b|\otimes\mathbb{I}_{S_{\mu}}=B_{\mu}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{S_{\mu}}

(304)

for a uniquely defined operator

B_{\mu}=\sum_{a,b}c_{ab}|a\rangle\langle b|\in\textup{End}(W_{\mu}).

(305)

We now evaluate terms in Eq. (
296
). Using
\sum_{\mu}P_{\mu}=\mathbb{I}
,
[B,P_{\mu}]=0
, and
[\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)},P_{\mu}]=0
, we obtain

\Tr\left(B\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right)=\sum_{\mu\vdash 4:\ell(\mu)\leq d}\Tr\left(P_{\mu}BP_{\mu}\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right).

(306)

On the
\mu
-block,

P_{\mu}BP_{\mu}=B_{\mu}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{S_{\mu}},\qquad\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\bigg|_{W_{\mu}\otimes S_{\mu}}=\mathbb{I}_{W_{\mu}}\otimes\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{\mu}(\omega^{-1}),

(307)

so that

\displaystyle\Tr\left(P_{\mu}BP_{\mu}\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right)

\displaystyle=\Tr\left((B_{\mu}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{S_{\mu}})(\mathbb{I}_{W_{\mu}}\otimes\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{\mu}(\omega^{-1}))\right)

(308)

\displaystyle=\Tr(B_\mu)\Tr\left(\Gamma_{S_{4}}^{\mu}(\omega^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\Tr(B_\mu)\chi_{\mu}(\omega^{-1}).

Summing over all blocks gives

\Tr\left(B\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right)=\sum_{\mu\vdash 4:\ell(\mu)\leq d}\Tr(B_\mu)\chi_{\mu}(\omega^{-1}).

(309)

Finally, note that

\Tr(BP_\mu)=\Tr(P_\mu BP_\mu)=\Tr(B_\mu\otimes\bI_{S_\mu})=d_{\mu}\Tr(B_\mu),

(310)

so equivalently

\Tr(B_\mu)=\frac{1}{d_{\mu}}\Tr(BP_\mu).

(311)

Then Eq. (
309
) becomes

\Tr\left(B\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right)=\sum_{\mu\vdash 4:\ell(\mu)\leq d}\frac{1}{d_{\mu}}\Tr(BP_\mu)\chi_{\mu}(\omega^{-1}).

(312)

Substituting this into the left-hand side of Eq. (
296
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d_{\lambda}}{24}\sum_{\omega\in S_{4}}\Tr\left(B\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right)\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}P_{\lambda}=\sum_{\mu\vdash 4:\ell(\mu)\leq d}\frac{1}{d_{\mu}}\Tr(BP_\mu)\left[\frac{d_{\lambda}}{24}\sum_{\omega\in S_{4}}\chi_{\mu}(\omega^{-1})\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}P_{\lambda}\right]=\sum_{\mu\vdash 4:\ell(\mu)\leq d}\frac{1}{d_{\mu}}\Tr(BP_\mu)P_{\mu}P_{\lambda}.

(313)

Notice that the projectors defined in Eq. (
286
) are orthogonal projections on
W_{\lambda}\otimes S_{\lambda}
,

P_{\mu}P_{\lambda}=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}P_{\lambda},

(314)

Eq. (
296
) then follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
13
.

Since
\rho^{\otimes 4}
is invariant under permutations of the four tensor factors, we have

\left[\rho^{\otimes 4},\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}\right]=0,\qquad\forall\omega\in S_{4}.

(315)

Because
Q
and
Q^{\perp}
both commute with all permutation operators in
S_{4}
(easily follows from Eq. (
281
)), we have

\left[\rho^{\otimes 4}Q,\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}\right]=0,\qquad\left[\rho^{\otimes 4}Q^{\perp},\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}\right]=0,\qquad\forall\omega\in S_{4}.

(316)

Take
A=\rho^{\otimes 4}
in Eq. (
294
), then each of the two
\omega
-sums can be simplified separately.
Apply Lemma
5
first with

B=\rho^{\otimes 4}Q
, this gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}d_{\lambda}\sum_{\omega\in S_{4}}\frac{1}{D_{\lambda}^{+}}\Tr\left(\rho^{\otimes 4}Q\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right)Q\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}P_{\lambda}=\frac{Q}{D_{\lambda}^{+}}\left[\frac{d_{\lambda}}{24}\sum_{\omega\in S_{4}}\Tr\left(\rho^{\otimes 4}Q\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right)\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}P_{\lambda}\right]=\frac{1}{D_{\lambda}^{+}}\frac{1}{d_{\lambda}}\Tr\left(\rho^{\otimes 4}QP_{\lambda}\right)QP_{\lambda}.

(317)

Likewise, apply Lemma
5
with

B=\rho^{\otimes 4}Q^{\perp}
,
we obtain

\frac{1}{24}d_{\lambda}\sum_{\omega\in S_{4}}\frac{1}{D_{\lambda}^{-}}\Tr\left(\rho^{\otimes 4}Q^{\perp}\mathbb{P}_{\omega^{-1}}^{(4)}\right)Q^{\perp}\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(4)}P_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{D_{\lambda}^{-}}\frac{1}{d_{\lambda}}\Tr\left(\rho^{\otimes 4}Q^{\perp}P_{\lambda}\right)Q^{\perp}P_{\lambda}.

(318)

Recall the definitions in Eq. (
288
),

\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(\rho)=\frac{1}{d_{\lambda}}\Tr\left(\rho^{\otimes 4}P_{\lambda}\right),\qquad\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\rho)=\frac{1}{d_{\lambda}}\Tr\left(\rho^{\otimes 4}QP_{\lambda}\right).

(319)

Since
Q^{\perp}=\mathbb{I}-Q
, we also have

\frac{1}{d_{\lambda}}\Tr\left(\rho^{\otimes 4}Q^{\perp}P_{\lambda}\right)=\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(\rho)-\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\rho).

(320)

Substituting Eqs. (
317
)–(
320
) into Eq. (
294
), we arrive at

\Phi_{\mathrm{Cl}_{n}}^{(4)}(\rho^{\otimes 4})=\sum_{\lambda\vdash 4:\ell(\lambda)\leq d}\left[\frac{\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\rho)}{D_{\lambda}^{+}}Q+\frac{\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(\rho)-\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\rho)}{D_{\lambda}^{-}}Q^{\perp}\right]P_{\lambda}.

(321)

And then

\displaystyle\norm{\rho}_{O,\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}^{4}=\sum_{\lambda\vdash 4:\ell(\lambda)\leq d}\left[\frac{\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\rho)}{D_{\lambda}^{+}}\Tr(O^{\ot 4}QP_\lambda)+\frac{\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(\rho)-\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\rho)}{D_{\lambda}^{-}}\Tr(O^{\ot 4}Q^\perp P_\lambda)\right],

(322)

where the terms with zero denominator should be omitted in the above summation.
Now by the definitions of
\widehat{p}_{\lambda}
and
\widehat{q}_{\lambda}
in Eq. (
288
),

\displaystyle\Tr(O^{\ot 4}QP_\lambda)

\displaystyle=d_{\lambda}\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(O),

(323)

\displaystyle\Tr(O^{\ot 4}Q^\perp P_\lambda)

\displaystyle=d_{\lambda}\left(\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(O)-\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(O)\right).

Substituting these identities proves Eq. (
291
).
∎

E.2
Coefficients for standard triangle witness and proof of Corollary
2

We then calculate the explicit coefficients related to
\widetilde{T}
that appear in the formula of
\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{T},\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}
.
For the witness induced by the triangle criterion
74

W=T=\frac{\mathbb{I}+X-Y+Z}{1+\sqrt{3}}\otimes\ket{0^{n-1}}\bra{0^{n-1}},\qquad\widetilde{W}=\widetilde{T}=\mathbb{I}-T,

(324)

its spectrum is

\operatorname{spec}(\widetilde{T})=\{0,3-\sqrt{3},1,\dots,1\},

(325)

where the eigenvalue
1
has multiplicity
d-2
with
d=2^{n}
, and we write its
k
th-order moment as

t_{k}=\Tr(\widetilde T^k)=d-2+(3-\sqrt{3})^{k},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}.

(326)

Especially, the first four moments are

\displaystyle t_{1}

\displaystyle=d+1-\sqrt{3},

\displaystyle t_{2}

\displaystyle=d+10-6\sqrt{3},

\displaystyle t_{3}

\displaystyle=d+52-30\sqrt{3},

\displaystyle t_{4}

\displaystyle=d+250-144\sqrt{3}.

(327)

Lemma 6

(Coefficient data for
\widetilde{T}
)
.

Write
\nu_{\lambda}=\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(\widetilde{T})
,
a_{\lambda}=\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\widetilde{T})
and
b_{\lambda}=\nu_{\lambda}-a_{\lambda}
, their explicit values are

\displaystyle\nu_{[4]}

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{1}^{4}+6t_{1}^{2}t_{2}+3t_{2}^{2}+8t_{1}t_{3}+6t_{4}}{24},

(328)

\displaystyle\nu_{[3,1]}

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{1}^{4}+2t_{1}^{2}t_{2}-t_{2}^{2}-2t_{4}}{8},

\displaystyle\nu_{[2,2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{1}^{4}+3t_{2}^{2}-4t_{1}t_{3}}{12},

\displaystyle\nu_{[2,1,1]}

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{1}^{4}-2t_{1}^{2}t_{2}-t_{2}^{2}+2t_{4}}{8},

\displaystyle\nu_{[1,1,1,1]}

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{1}^{4}-6t_{1}^{2}t_{2}+3t_{2}^{2}+8t_{1}t_{3}-6t_{4}}{24},

where values of
t_{k}
are given in Eq. (
327
).
Moreover,

\displaystyle a_{[4]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\left(d^{2}+(7-4\sqrt{3})d+86-48\sqrt{3}+\frac{800-464\sqrt{3}}{d}\right),

(329)

\displaystyle a_{[2,2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\left(d^{2}+(4-4\sqrt{3})d+23-12\sqrt{3}+\frac{-70+40\sqrt{3}}{d}\right),

\displaystyle a_{[1,1,1,1]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\left(d^{2}+(1-4\sqrt{3})d-34+24\sqrt{3}+\frac{56-32\sqrt{3}}{d}\right),

\displaystyle a_{[3,1]}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle a_{[2,1,1]}

\displaystyle=0.

Proof of Lemma
6
.

The formulas for
\nu_{\lambda}
follow immediately from the cycle traces of
\widetilde{T}^{\otimes 4}
,

\displaystyle p_{[1,1,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})

\displaystyle=t_{1}^{4},

\displaystyle p_{[2,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})

\displaystyle=t_{1}^{2}t_{2},

\displaystyle p_{[2,2]}(\widetilde{T})

\displaystyle=t_{2}^{2},

\displaystyle p_{[3,1]}(\widetilde{T})

\displaystyle=t_{1}t_{3},

\displaystyle p_{[4]}(\widetilde{T})

\displaystyle=t_{4},

(330)

similar to Eq. (
289
). It therefore remains to compute the
Q
-sector coefficients
a_{\lambda}
.

Write

\widetilde{T}=\mathbb{I}-A\otimes\Pi_{0},\qquad A=\frac{\mathbb{I}+X-Y+Z}{1+\sqrt{3}},\qquad\Pi_{0}=|0^{n-1}\rangle\langle 0^{n-1}|.

(331)

Let

P=p\otimes P_{\mathrm{tail}},\qquad p\in\{\mathbb{I},X,Y,Z\},

(332)

where
P_{\mathrm{tail}}
is a Hermitian Pauli string acting on the last
n-1
qubits. Since

\Pi_{0}P_{\mathrm{tail}}\Pi_{0}=\langle 0^{n-1}|P_{\mathrm{tail}}|0^{n-1}\rangle\Pi_{0},

(333)

we divide
P_{\mathrm{tail}}
into three cases:

•

Class (i):

P_{\mathrm{tail}}=\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(n-1)}
. This class contains one element.

•

Class (ii):

P_{\mathrm{tail}}\in\{\mathbb{I},Z\}^{\otimes(n-1)}\setminus\{\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(n-1)}\}
. This class contains
d/2-1
elements.

•

Class (iii):
all remaining Pauli strings
P_{\mathrm{tail}}
. This class contains
d(d-2)/4
elements.

For each class, we compute the terms

\tau_{k}(P)=\Tr\left((\widetilde{T}P)^{k}\right),\qquad k=1,2,3,4,

(334)

that appear in
\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\widetilde{T})
.

Step 1: Class (i).

If
P_{\mathrm{tail}}=\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(n-1)}
, then

\widetilde{T}P=p\otimes(\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{0})+(p-Ap)\otimes\Pi_{0}.

(335)

Because
(\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{0})\Pi_{0}=\Pi_{0}(\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{0})=0
, the two summands act on orthogonal sectors and therefore

(\widetilde{T}P)^{k}=p^{k}\otimes(\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{0})+(p-Ap)^{k}\otimes\Pi_{0}.

(336)

Hence

\tau_{k}(P)=\Tr(p^k)\Tr(\bI-\Pi_0)+\Tr\left((p-Ap)^{k}\right)\Tr(\Pi_0).

(337)

Now

\Tr(\bI-\Pi_0)=\frac{d}{2}-1,\qquad\Tr(\Pi_0)=1.

(338)

Moreover, a direct one-qubit calculation (cf. Eq. (
326
)) gives

\Tr\left((\mathbb{I}-A)^{k}\right)=\left.t_{k}\right|_{d=2}=\left(3-\sqrt{3}\right)^{k},

(339)

\displaystyle\Tr(X-AX)

\displaystyle=\Tr(Z-AZ)=1-\sqrt{3},

(340)

\displaystyle\Tr\left((X-AX)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\Tr\left((Z-AZ)^{2}\right)=4-2\sqrt{3},

\displaystyle\Tr\left((X-AX)^{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\Tr\left((Z-AZ)^{3}\right)=10-6\sqrt{3},

\displaystyle\Tr\left((X-AX)^{4}\right)

\displaystyle=\Tr\left((Z-AZ)^{4}\right)=28-16\sqrt{3},

and

\displaystyle\Tr(Y-AY)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{3}-1,

(341)

\displaystyle\Tr\left((Y-AY)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=4-2\sqrt{3},

\displaystyle\Tr\left((Y-AY)^{3}\right)

\displaystyle=-10+6\sqrt{3},

\displaystyle\Tr\left((Y-AY)^{4}\right)

\displaystyle=28-16\sqrt{3}.

Inserting above equations to Eq. (
337
), we obtain

\displaystyle(\tau_{1}(P),\tau_{2}(P),\tau_{3}(P),\tau_{4}(P))

\displaystyle=(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}),

\displaystyle p=\mathbb{I},

(342)

\displaystyle(\tau_{1}(P),\tau_{2}(P),\tau_{3}(P),\tau_{4}(P))

\displaystyle=(1-\sqrt{3},\ d+2-2\sqrt{3},\ 10-6\sqrt{3},\ d+26-16\sqrt{3}),

\displaystyle p=X,Z,

\displaystyle(\tau_{1}(P),\tau_{2}(P),\tau_{3}(P),\tau_{4}(P))

\displaystyle=(\sqrt{3}-1,\ d+2-2\sqrt{3},\ -10+6\sqrt{3},\ d+26-16\sqrt{3}),

\displaystyle p=Y.

Step 2: Class (ii).

Now let

P_{\mathrm{tail}}\in\{\mathbb{I},Z\}^{\otimes(n-1)}\setminus\{\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(n-1)}\}.

(343)

Then
P_{\mathrm{tail}}
is diagonal in the computational basis, it has eigenvalue
+1
on
|0^{n-1}\rangle
, and it is traceless. Hence

P_{\mathrm{tail}}\Pi_{0}=\Pi_{0}P_{\mathrm{tail}}=\Pi_{0},\qquad\Tr(P_{\mathrm{tail}})=0.

(344)

Therefore

\widetilde{T}P=p\otimes(P_{\mathrm{tail}}-\Pi_{0})+(p-Ap)\otimes\Pi_{0}.

(345)

Again, the two tensor factors act on orthogonal sectors because

(P_{\mathrm{tail}}-\Pi_{0})\Pi_{0}=\Pi_{0}(P_{\mathrm{tail}}-\Pi_{0})=0.

(346)

Thus

(\widetilde{T}P)^{k}=p^{k}\otimes(P_{\mathrm{tail}}-\Pi_{0})^{k}+(p-Ap)^{k}\otimes\Pi_{0}

(347)

and

\tau_{k}(P)=\Tr(p^k)\Tr\left((P_{\mathrm{tail}}-\Pi_{0})^{k}\right)+\Tr\left((p-Ap)^{k}\right)\Tr(\Pi_0).

(348)

Since
P_{\mathrm{tail}}^{2}=\mathbb{I}
and
P_{\mathrm{tail}}\Pi_{0}=\Pi_{0}
, we have

(P_{\mathrm{tail}}-\Pi_{0})^{2}=\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{0},

(349)

and since
\Tr(P_{\mathrm{tail}})=0
,

\Tr(P_{\mathrm{tail}}-\Pi_0)=-1.

(350)

Consequently,

\Tr\left((P_{\mathrm{tail}}-\Pi_{0})^{2m}\right)=\frac{d}{2}-1,\qquad\Tr\left((P_{\mathrm{tail}}-\Pi_{0})^{2m+1}\right)=-1.

(351)

Inserting Eqs. (
339
)-(
341
) to Eq. (
348
), we have

\displaystyle(\tau_{1}(P),\tau_{2}(P),\tau_{3}(P),\tau_{4}(P))

\displaystyle=(1-\sqrt{3},\ t_{2},\ 52-30\sqrt{3},\ t_{4}),

\displaystyle p=\mathbb{I},

(352)

\displaystyle(\tau_{1}(P),\tau_{2}(P),\tau_{3}(P),\tau_{4}(P))

\displaystyle=(1-\sqrt{3},\ d+2-2\sqrt{3},\ 10-6\sqrt{3},\ d+26-16\sqrt{3}),

\displaystyle p=X,Z,

\displaystyle(\tau_{1}(P),\tau_{2}(P),\tau_{3}(P),\tau_{4}(P))

\displaystyle=(\sqrt{3}-1,\ d+2-2\sqrt{3},\ -10+6\sqrt{3},\ d+26-16\sqrt{3}),

\displaystyle p=Y.

Step 3: Class (iii).

Suppose now that
P_{\mathrm{tail}}
contains at least one
X
or
Y
factor. Then

\Pi_{0}P_{\mathrm{tail}}\Pi_{0}=0.

(353)

Set

|\beta\rangle=P_{\mathrm{tail}}|0^{n-1}\rangle.

(354)

Since
P_{\mathrm{tail}}
contains at least one
X
or
Y
factor, the computational-basis vector
|0^{n-1}\rangle
is mapped to a basis vector orthogonal to itself, and therefore

\langle 0^{n-1}|\beta\rangle=0.

(355)

Hence the
(n-1)
-qubit Hilbert space decomposes as

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{tail}}=\mathrm{span}\{|0^{n-1}\rangle,|\beta\rangle\}\oplus\mathcal{K},

(356)

with

\dim\mathcal{K}=\frac{d}{2}-2.

(357)

Because
P_{\mathrm{tail}}
is Hermitian and unitary, it preserves this decomposition: indeed,

P_{\mathrm{tail}}|0^{n-1}\rangle=|\beta\rangle,\qquad P_{\mathrm{tail}}|\beta\rangle=P_{\mathrm{tail}}^{2}|0^{n-1}\rangle=|0^{n-1}\rangle,

(358)

so
\mathrm{span}\{|0^{n-1}\rangle,|\beta\rangle\}
is invariant, and for any
|v\rangle\in\mathcal{K}
one has

\langle 0^{n-1}|P_{\mathrm{tail}}|v\rangle=\langle P_{\mathrm{tail}}0^{n-1}|v\rangle=\langle\beta|v\rangle=0,

(359)

and similarly

\langle\beta|P_{\mathrm{tail}}|v\rangle=\langle P_{\mathrm{tail}}\beta|v\rangle=\langle 0^{n-1}|v\rangle=0.

(360)

Thus
P_{\mathrm{tail}}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
.

Now consider the full
n
-qubit space

\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{tail}}=\left(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathrm{span}\{|0^{n-1}\rangle,|\beta\rangle\}\right)\oplus(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{K}).

(361)

On the second summand, the projector
\Pi_{0}=|0^{n-1}\rangle\langle 0^{n-1}|
vanishes identically, because

\Pi_{0}|v\rangle=0,\qquad\forall|v\rangle\in\mathcal{K}.

(362)

Since
P_{\mathrm{tail}}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
, it follows that

\Pi_{0}P_{\mathrm{tail}}|v\rangle=0,\qquad\forall|v\rangle\in\mathcal{K}.

(363)

Therefore, on
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{K}
,

\widetilde{T}P=(\mathbb{I}-A\otimes\Pi_{0})(p\otimes P_{\mathrm{tail}})=p\otimes P_{\mathrm{tail}},

(364)

because the term
Ap\otimes\Pi_{0}P_{\mathrm{tail}}
is zero there.

The dimension of this subspace is

\dim(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{K})=2\left(\frac{d}{2}-2\right)=d-4.

(365)

Moreover,

(p\otimes P_{\mathrm{tail}})^{2}=p^{2}\otimes P_{\mathrm{tail}}^{2}=\mathbb{I},

(366)

so every even power acts as the identity on
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{K}
. Hence its contribution to the even traces is exactly

\Tr_{\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{K}}\left((p\otimes P_{\mathrm{tail}})^{2m}\right)=d-4.

(367)

For odd powers, one has

(p\otimes P_{\mathrm{tail}})^{2m+1}=p\otimes P_{\mathrm{tail}},

(368)

whose trace on
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{K}
vanishes. Indeed, if
p\in\{X,Y,Z\}
then
\Tr(p)=0
, while if
p=\mathbb{I}
, the operator
P_{\mathrm{tail}}
is traceless on the full tail space and also traceless on
\mathrm{span}\{|0^{n-1}\rangle,|\beta\rangle\}
, hence it is traceless on
\mathcal{K}
as well. Therefore

\Tr_{\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{K}}\left((p\otimes P_{\mathrm{tail}})^{2m+1}\right)=0.

(369)

In summary, the orthogonal-complement sector contributes
d-4
to every even trace and
0
to every odd trace.

On the two-dimensional subspace
\mathrm{span}\{|0^{n-1}\rangle,|\beta\rangle\}
, with respect to the ordered basis
\{|0^{n-1}\rangle,|\beta\rangle\}
,
we have

p\otimes P_{\mathrm{tail}}\sim\begin{bmatrix}0&p\\
p&0\end{bmatrix},\qquad Ap\otimes\Pi_{0}P_{\mathrm{tail}}\sim\begin{bmatrix}0&Ap\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}.

(370)

Hence

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}P

\displaystyle\sim\begin{bmatrix}0&p-Ap\\
p&0\end{bmatrix},

(371)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{T}P)^{2}

\displaystyle\sim\begin{bmatrix}\mathbb{I}-A&0\\
0&\mathbb{I}-pAp\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle(\widetilde{T}P)^{3}

\displaystyle\sim\begin{bmatrix}0&(p-Ap)(\mathbb{I}-pAp)\\
p(\mathbb{I}-A)&0\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle(\widetilde{T}P)^{4}

\displaystyle\sim\begin{bmatrix}(\mathbb{I}-A)^{2}&0\\
0&(\mathbb{I}-pAp)^{2}\end{bmatrix}.

Since
p^{2}=\mathbb{I}
, the matrices
A
and
pAp
are similar, and therefore

\Tr\left((\mathbb{I}-pAp)^{m}\right)=\Tr\left((\mathbb{I}-A)^{m}\right)\qquad\textup{for all }m\geq 1.

(372)

Using Eq. (
369
), it follows that the odd traces vanish,

\tau_{1}(P)=\tau_{3}(P)=0,

(373)

while the even traces (using Eq. (
367
)) are

\displaystyle\tau_{2}(P)=(d-4)+2\Tr(\bI-A)=d+2-2\sqrt{3},

(374)

and

\displaystyle\tau_{4}(P)=(d-4)+2\Tr\left((\mathbb{I}-A)^{2}\right)=d+20-12\sqrt{3}.

(375)

Thus

(\tau_{1}(P),\tau_{2}(P),\tau_{3}(P),\tau_{4}(P))=(0,\ d+2-2\sqrt{3},\ 0,\ d+20-12\sqrt{3})

(376)

for all
p\in\{\mathbb{I},X,Y,Z\}
.

Step 4: Evaluation of the five
Q
-sector invariants.

We now substitute the above traces into Eq. (
282
).

For
q_{[1,1,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})
, only Classes (i) (Eq. (
342
)) and (ii) (Eq. (
352
)) contribute:

\displaystyle q_{[1,1,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\left[t_{1}^{4}+3(1-\sqrt{3})^{4}+4\left(\frac{d}{2}-1\right)(1-\sqrt{3})^{4}\right]

(377)

\displaystyle=d^{2}+(4-4\sqrt{3})d+(24-12\sqrt{3})+\frac{96-56\sqrt{3}}{d}.

Next,

\displaystyle q_{[2,2]}(\widetilde{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\left[t_{2}^{2}+3(d+2-2\sqrt{3})^{2}+\left(\frac{d}{2}-1\right)\left(t_{2}^{2}+3(d+2-2\sqrt{3})^{2}\right)+d(d-2)(d+2-2\sqrt{3})^{2}\right]

(378)

\displaystyle=d^{2}+(4-4\sqrt{3})d+(24-12\sqrt{3})+\frac{96-56\sqrt{3}}{d}.

Hence

q_{[1,1,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})=q_{[2,2]}(\widetilde{T}).

(379)

For
q_{[2,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})
, only Classes (i) and (ii) contribute:

\displaystyle q_{[2,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\left[t_{2}t_{1}^{2}+3(d+2-2\sqrt{3})(1-\sqrt{3})^{2}+\left(\frac{d}{2}-1\right)\left(t_{2}(1-\sqrt{3})^{2}+3(d+2-2\sqrt{3})(1-\sqrt{3})^{2}\right)\right]

(380)

\displaystyle=d+(20-12\sqrt{3})+\frac{124-72\sqrt{3}}{d}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle q_{[4]}(\widetilde{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\left[t_{4}+3(d+26-16\sqrt{3})+\left(\frac{d}{2}-1\right)\left(t_{4}+3(d+26-16\sqrt{3})\right)+d(d-2)(d+20-12\sqrt{3})\right]

(381)

\displaystyle=d+(20-12\sqrt{3})+\frac{124-72\sqrt{3}}{d}.

Hence

q_{[2,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})=q_{[4]}(\widetilde{T}).

(382)

Finally,

\displaystyle q_{[3,1]}(\widetilde{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\left[t_{3}t_{1}+3(10-6\sqrt{3})(1-\sqrt{3})+\left(\frac{d}{2}-1\right)\left((52-30\sqrt{3})(1-\sqrt{3})+3(10-6\sqrt{3})(1-\sqrt{3})\right)\right]

(383)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{166-96\sqrt{3}}{d}.

Step 5: Computation of
a_{\lambda}
.

Substituting the above formulas into Eq. (
288
), and using

q_{[1,1,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})=q_{[2,2]}(\widetilde{T}),\qquad q_{[2,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})=q_{[4]}(\widetilde{T}),

(384)

we obtain (omitting
\widetilde{T}
for simplicity)

\displaystyle a_{[4]}

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{[1,1,1,1]}+6q_{[2,1,1]}+3q_{[2,2]}+8q_{[3,1]}+6q_{[4]}}{24}

(385)

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{[1,1,1,1]}+3q_{[2,1,1]}+2q_{[3,1]}}{6},

\displaystyle a_{[2,2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{2q_{[1,1,1,1]}+6q_{[2,2]}-8q_{[3,1]}}{24}=\frac{q_{[1,1,1,1]}-q_{[3,1]}}{3},

\displaystyle a_{[1,1,1,1]}

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{[1,1,1,1]}-6q_{[2,1,1]}+3q_{[2,2]}+8q_{[3,1]}-6q_{[4]}}{24}

\displaystyle=\frac{q_{[1,1,1,1]}-3q_{[2,1,1]}+2q_{[3,1]}}{6},

\displaystyle a_{[3,1]}

\displaystyle=\frac{3q_{[1,1,1,1]}+6q_{[2,1,1]}-3q_{[2,2]}-6q_{[4]}}{24}=0,

\displaystyle a_{[2,1,1]}

\displaystyle=\frac{3q_{[1,1,1,1]}-6q_{[2,1,1]}-3q_{[2,2]}+6q_{[4]}}{24}=0.

Substituting the explicit expressions for
q_{[1,1,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})
,
q_{[2,1,1]}(\widetilde{T})
, and
q_{[3,1]}(\widetilde{T})
gives exactly Eq. (
329
).
∎

As a special case, when the input state is pure, the coefficients and the character transforms (Eq. (
289
) and Eq. (
290
)) largely simplify. As shown in the following corollary (reformulated from Corollary
2
in the main text),
\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{T},\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}^{4}
becomes a function linear in the Pauli fourth moment of
\rho
, which relates directly to the stabilizer 2-Rényi entropy
71
. We also provide an explicit formula for the stabilizer threshold
C_{\widetilde{T},\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}
.

Corollary 8

(Pure-state reduction and the stabilizer threshold)
.

Let
\psi=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
be pure and define the Pauli fourth moment

\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\psi)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}|\langle\psi|P|\psi\rangle|^{4}.

(386)

Then

\displaystyle\norm{\psi}_{\widetilde{T},\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}^{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{[4]}}{D_{[4]}^{+}}\frac{\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\psi)}{d}+\frac{b_{[4]}}{D_{[4]}^{-}}\left(1-\frac{\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\psi)}{d}\right)

(387)

\displaystyle=\frac{24(7-4\sqrt{3})(d-8)}{d(d-1)(d+1)(d+2)(d+4)}\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\psi)+C_{4},

where

C_{4}=\frac{-3200+1856\sqrt{3}+3400d-1968\sqrt{3}d+862d^{2}-496\sqrt{3}d^{2}+103d^{3}-60\sqrt{3}d^{3}+10d^{4}-4\sqrt{3}d^{4}+d^{5}}{d(d-1)(d+1)(d+2)(d+4)}

(388)

is a constant.
In particular, every pure stabilizer state
\phi
satisfies
\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\phi)=1
. Setting

\Delta_{4}=d(d+1)(d+2)(d+4),

(389)

one obtains

\displaystyle C_{\widetilde{T},\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}^{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{[4]}}{dD_{[4]}^{+}}+\frac{(1-1/d)b_{[4]}}{D_{[4]}^{-}}

(390)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Delta_{4}}\left[d^{4}+(11-4\sqrt{3})d^{3}+(114-64\sqrt{3})d^{2}+(976-560\sqrt{3})d+4544-2624\sqrt{3}\right].

Proof.

Inserting
r_{2}=r_{3}=r_{4}=1
to Eq. (
289
), we have

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{[4]}(\psi)

\displaystyle=1,

(391)

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(\psi)

\displaystyle=0,\quad\textup{for }\lambda\neq[4].

Now set
m_{P}=\langle\psi|P|\psi\rangle
. Since
\psi P\psi=m_{P}\psi
, all five
Q
-sector invariants (cf.Eq. (
283
)) reduce to the same value,

\displaystyle q_{[1,1,1,1]}(\psi)

\displaystyle=q_{[2,1,1]}(\psi)=q_{[2,2]}(\psi)

(392)

\displaystyle=q_{[3,1]}(\psi)=q_{[4]}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}m_{P}^{4}=\frac{\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\psi)}{d}.

Applying Eq. (
290
) and using the character orthogonality relations gives

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{[4]}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\psi)}{d},

(393)

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\psi)

\displaystyle=0\quad\textup{for }\lambda\neq[4].

Substituting these identities into Theorem
13
proves Eq. (
387
).

If
\phi
is a pure stabilizer state, then exactly
d
Hermitian Paulis have
expectation value
1
on
\phi
, while all others have expectation value
0
, hence

\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\phi)=1
. Since the maximum of WE
4
-norm over the stabilizer
polytope
is attained at an arbitrary pure stabilizer state by Observation
1
,

C_{\widetilde{T},\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}^{4}=\norm{\phi}_{\widetilde{T},\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}^{4}=\frac{a_{[4]}}{dD_{[4]}^{+}}+\frac{(1-1/d)b_{[4]}}{D_{[4]}^{-}}.

(394)

Using the results in Lemma
6
, we obtain Eq. (
390
).
∎

E.3
Proof of Corollary
3

We then consider the WE
4
-criterion constructed by a seed operator chosen as the rank-
1
projector onto a
pure
n
-qubit state vector
\ket{\Phi}
with high magic. We first prove the existence of such a state and
then calculate the coefficients introduced in Theorem
6
and
Theorem
13
.

Proof of Corollary
3
.

Denote the fifth root of unity as
\omega_{5}=e^{i2\pi/5}
and for
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d-1})\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}^{d-1}
define the normalized
n
-qubit pure state

\ket{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}=d^{-1/4}\ket{0^n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d+\sqrt{d}}}\sum_{x=1}^{d-1}\omega_{5}^{m_{x}}\ket{x}.

(395)

We then show the average evaluation of
\mathfrak{P}_{2}
of states in this ensemble
\left\{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\middle\mid\mathbf{m}\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}^{d-1}\right\}
is strictly smaller the
4/(d+3)
. So that there must exist some
\mathbf{m}
, such that

\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}})<\frac{4}{d+3}.

(396)

Choose each entry
m_{x}
of
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d-1})
independently and uniformly from
\{0,1,2,3,4\}
. We parametrize an
n
-qubit Pauli operator ignoring phase as
X^{a}Z^{b}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
, then

\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}})=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{a,b\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\absolutevalue{\bra{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}X^aZ^b\ket{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}}^{4}.

(397)

•

If
a=0
, then
\bra{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}Z^{0}\ket{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}=1
. For
b\neq 0
, using

\sum_{x\neq 0}(-1)^{b\cdot x}=-1
, we have

\bra{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}Z^{b}\ket{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}=d^{-1/2}-\frac{1}{d+\sqrt{d}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}+1}.

(398)

Hence the total
a=0
contribution to the sum in Eq. (
397
) is

1+\frac{d-1}{(\sqrt{d}+1)^{4}}.

(399)

•

Now fix
a\neq 0
and notice that

\bra{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}X^{a}Z^{b}\ket{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}}(-1)^{b\cdot x}\langle\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}|x+a\rangle\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{M}_{a}}\Lambda_{e},

(400)

where we pair the computational basis strings as unordered pairs

e=\{x,x+a\}
and denote the collection of all such pairs as
\mathcal{M}_{a}
. Then the contribution of
e
in the summation has the form

\displaystyle\Lambda_{e}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b\cdot x}\langle\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}|x+a\rangle\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle+(-1)^{b\cdot(x+a)}\langle\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}|x\rangle\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle

(401)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b\cdot x}\times\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}\times\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}\times\left(\omega_{5}^{m_{x}-m_{x+a}}+(-1)^{b\cdot a}\omega_{5}^{m_{x+a}-m_{x}}\right),

where we set
m_{0}=0
and use

\langle y|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle=\absolutevalue{\langle y|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}\omega_{5}^{m_{y}}
.
For each fixed
e=\{x,x+a\}
, the phase difference
m_{x}-m_{x+a}
is uniformly distributed in
\mathbb{Z}_{5}
. Using the fifth-root identity
\sum_{r=0}^{4}\omega_{5}^{\ell r}=0
for
\ell\neq 0
,
we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\omega_{5}^{\ell(m_{x}-m_{x+a})}\right]=0,\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\omega_{5}^{\ell(m_{x+a}-m_{x})}\right]=0,\qquad\ell=1,2,3,4.

(402)

We now compute the one-pair moments explicitly. First,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\Lambda_{e}\right]=(-1)^{b\cdot x}\times\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}\times\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}\times\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\omega_{5}^{m_{x}-m_{x+a}}\right]+(-1)^{b\cdot a}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\omega_{5}^{m_{x+a}-m_{x}}\right]\right)=0.

(403)

For the second absolute moment,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Lambda_e}^{2}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{
\omega_5^{m_x-m_{x+a}}+(-1)^{b\cdot a}\omega_5^{m_{x+a}-m_x}
}^{2}

(404)

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\left(2+(-1)^{b\cdot a}\omega_{5}^{2(m_{x}-m_{x+a})}+(-1)^{b\cdot a}\omega_{5}^{2(m_{x+a}-m_{x})}\right).

Taking the expectation and using Eq. (
402
) with
\ell=2
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\absolutevalue{\Lambda_e}^{2}\right]=2\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}.

(405)

For the second complex moment,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{e}^{2}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\left(\omega_{5}^{m_{x}-m_{x+a}}+(-1)^{b\cdot a}\omega_{5}^{m_{x+a}-m_{x}}\right)^{2}

(406)

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\left(\omega_{5}^{2(m_{x}-m_{x+a})}+2(-1)^{b\cdot a}+\omega_{5}^{2(m_{x+a}-m_{x})}\right).

Thus

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\Lambda_{e}^{2}\right]=2(-1)^{b\cdot a}\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}.

(407)

Finally, for the fourth absolute moment, by Eq. (
404
):

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Lambda_e}^{4}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}\left(2+(-1)^{b\cdot a}\omega_{5}^{2(m_{x}-m_{x+a})}+(-1)^{b\cdot a}\omega_{5}^{2(m_{x+a}-m_{x})}\right)^{2}

(408)

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}\left(6+4(-1)^{b\cdot a}\omega_{5}^{2(m_{x}-m_{x+a})}+4(-1)^{b\cdot a}\omega_{5}^{2(m_{x+a}-m_{x})}+\omega_{5}^{4(m_{x}-m_{x+a})}+\omega_{5}^{4(m_{x+a}-m_{x})}\right).

The terms with powers
2
and
4
vanish after averaging, so

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\absolutevalue{\Lambda_e}^{4}\right]=6\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}.

(409)

Distinct unordered pairs in
\mathcal{M}_{a}
use disjoint phase variables, hence the corresponding pair contributions are independent. We now expand the fourth absolute moment of Eq. (
400
). Since

\absolutevalue{\sum_{e\in\mathcal M_a}\Lambda_e}^{4}=\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{M}_{a}}\Lambda_{e}\right)^{2}\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{M}_{a}}\overline{\Lambda_{e}}\right)^{2},

(410)

all terms in which some pair contribution appears only once vanish by Eq. (
403
). The surviving terms are exactly

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\absolutevalue{\sum_{e\in\mathcal M_a}\Lambda_e}^{4}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{M}_{a}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\absolutevalue{\Lambda_e}^{4}\right]+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e,f\in\mathcal{M}_{a}\\
e\neq f\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\Lambda_{e}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\overline{\Lambda_{f}}^{2}\right]+4\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e,f\in\mathcal{M}_{a}\\
e<f\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\absolutevalue{\Lambda_e}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\absolutevalue{\Lambda_f}^{2}\right].

(411)

Here
e<f
denotes an arbitrary fixed ordering of the finite set
\mathcal{M}_{a}
and the final value is independent of that ordering. Substituting Eq. (
405
), Eq. (
407
) and Eq. (
409
) into Eq. (
411
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\absolutevalue{\bra{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}X^aZ^b\ket{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}}^{4}\right]=

\displaystyle 6\sum_{\{x,x+a\}}\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}

(412)

\displaystyle+24\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{x,x+a\}<\{y,y+a\}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle y|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle y+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}.

The coefficient
24
is the sum of
8
from Eq. (
407
) and
16
from Eq. (
405
). Noticing that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{x,x+a\}<\{y,y+a\}\end{subarray}}\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle y|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle y+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}

(413)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{\{x,x+a\}}\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\{x,x+a\}}\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}

we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\absolutevalue{\bra{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}X^aZ^b\ket{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}}^{4}\right]

(414)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 12\left(\sum_{\{x,x+a\}}\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\right)^{2}-6\sum_{\{x,x+a\}}\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}.

For the state vector
\ket{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}
in Eq. (
395
), exactly one unordered pair contains
0
and
a
, while the other
d/2-1
pairs contain two nonzero strings. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{\{x,x+a\}}\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d(\sqrt{d}+1)}+\frac{d/2-1}{d(\sqrt{d}+1)^{2}}

(415)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{d}+2}{2\sqrt{d}(\sqrt{d}+1)^{2}},

while

\displaystyle\sum_{\{x,x+a\}}\absolutevalue{\langle x|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}\absolutevalue{\langle x+a|\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}\rangle}^{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}(\sqrt{d}+1)^{2}}+\frac{d/2-1}{d^{2}(\sqrt{d}+1)^{4}}

(416)

\displaystyle=\frac{3\sqrt{d}+4}{2d^{3/2}(\sqrt{d}+1)^{4}}.

Substituting Eq. (
415
) and Eq. (
416
) into Eq. (
414
) yields, for every
a\neq 0
and every
b
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\absolutevalue{\bra{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}X^aZ^b\ket{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}}^{4}\right]=\frac{3(d^{3/2}+4d+\sqrt{d}-4)}{d^{3/2}(\sqrt{d}+1)^{4}}.

(417)

Combining Eq. (
399
) and Eq. (
417
) in Eq. (
397
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\left[1+\frac{d-1}{(\sqrt{d}+1)^{4}}+d(d-1)\frac{3(d^{3/2}+4d+\sqrt{d}-4)}{d^{3/2}(\sqrt{d}+1)^{4}}\right]

(418)

\displaystyle=\frac{4d^{2}+12d^{3/2}-5d-15\sqrt{d}+12}{d^{3/2}(\sqrt{d}+1)^{3}}.

A direct simplification gives the gap

\frac{4}{d+3}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{m}}\left[\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}})\right]=\frac{(\sqrt{d}-1)(5d^{3/2}-12d-9\sqrt{d}+36)}{d^{3/2}(\sqrt{d}+1)^{3}(d+3)}>0.

(419)

The denominator is positive and
\sqrt{d}>1
. If
n=1
, then
\sqrt{d}=\sqrt{2}
and

5d^{3/2}-12d-9\sqrt{d}+36=12+\sqrt{2}>0
. If
n\geq 2
, then
\sqrt{d}\geq 2
; the polynomial
5x^{3}-12x^{2}-9x+36
is increasing for
x\geq 2
because its derivative
15x^{2}-24x-9
is positive there, and its value at
x=2
is
10
. Thus the finite average in Eq. (
418
) is strictly below
4/(d+3)
. Especially, we could search lexicographically over
\mathbf{m}\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}^{d-1}
for a concrete
\ket{\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}}}
such that
\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi_{n,\mathbf{m}})<4/(d+3)
.

We then calculate
\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(\Phi)
and
\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\Phi)
in Eq. (
291
). Notice that
\Phi
is a pure state, following a similar argument in deriving Eq. (
391
) and Eq. (
393
), we have

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{[4]}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=1,\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{[4]}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi)}{d},

(420)

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{\lambda}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{\lambda}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=0\quad\textup{for }\lambda\neq[4].

According to the dimensions related to
\lambda=[4]
in Table
1
, we recover the WE
4
-norm in Eq. (
67
) in the main text:

\norm{\rho}_{\Phi,\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}^{4}=\frac{6\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi)}{d(d+1)(d+2)}\widehat{q}_{[4]}(\rho)+\frac{24(d-\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi))}{d(d-1)(d+1)(d+2)(d+4)}\left(\widehat{p}_{[4]}(\rho)-\widehat{q}_{[4]}(\rho)\right).

(421)

And its pure-state reduction is

\norm{\psi}_{\Phi,\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}^{4}=\frac{6\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi)\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\psi)}{d^{2}(d+1)(d+2)}+\frac{24(d-\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi))(d-\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\psi))}{d^{2}(d-1)(d+1)(d+2)(d+4)}.

(422)

For a pure stabilizer state
\phi=\outerproduct{\phi}{\phi}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\phi)=1
, so the threshold value for all stabilizer states is given by

C_{\Phi,\mathrm{Cl}_{n},4}^{4}=\frac{6(\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\Phi)+4)}{d(d+1)(d+2)(d+4)},

(423)

which is Eq. (
68
). Then Eq. (
69
) follows directly from the above two equations. For an
n
-qubit pure state
\psi
,
\mathfrak{P}_{2}(\psi)\leq 1
, with equality if and only if
\psi
is a stabilizer state
71
, which proves Eq. (
70
).

∎

Appendix F
Fermionic non-Gaussianity

F.1
Proof of Proposition
2

In the resource theory of fermionic non-Gaussianity, it is convenient to introduce Majorana operators. They become products of Pauli operators under Jordan-Wigner transformation.

Definition 2

(Majorana operators)
.

For an
n
-mode fermionic state, which corresponds to an
n
-qubit state by Jordan-Wigner transformation, the set of
2n
Majorana operators
\left\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\cdots,\gamma_{2n}\right\}
is defined as
\forall k\in[n]
,

\gamma_{2k-1}\coloneqq\left(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k-1}Z_{j}\right)X_{k},\quad\gamma_{2k}\coloneqq\left(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k-1}Z_{j}\right)Y_{k}.

(424)

For each
j\in[2n]
, the Majorana operator
\gamma_{j}
satisfies

\gamma_{j}=\gamma_{j}^{\dagger},\quad\Tr(\gamma_j)=0,\quad\gamma_{j}^{2}=\mathbb{I}.

(425)

It is also easy to verify that for any
j,k\in[2n]
, we have

\{\gamma_{j},\gamma_{k}\}=\delta_{j,k}\cdot 2\mathbb{I},\quad\Tr(\gamma_j^\dagger\gamma_k)=\delta_{j,k}\cdot 2^{n}.

(426)

Definition 3

(Majorana products)
.

For a non-empty subset
S=\left\{\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{\absolutevalue{S}}\right\}
of
[2n]
, with
1\leq\mu_{1}<\cdots<\mu_{\absolutevalue{S}}\leq 2n
, define

\gamma_{S}\coloneqq\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\absolutevalue{S}}\gamma_{\mu_{j}}.

(427)

Specially, we define
\gamma_{\emptyset}=\mathbb{I}
. We call
\absolutevalue{S}
as the degree of
\gamma_{S}
.

Notice that for subsets
S,S^{\prime}\subseteq[2n]
, Majorana products are orthogonal

\Tr(\gamma_S^\dagger\gamma_{S'})=\delta_{S,S^{\prime}}\cdot 2^{n}

(428)

w.r.t. the
Hilbert–Schmidt inner product, where the Hermitian conjugate satisfies

\gamma_{S}^{\dagger}=(-1)^{\absolutevalue{S}(\absolutevalue{S}-1)/2}\gamma_{S}.

(429)

Since
\gamma_{S}
may not be Hermitian,
we also denote the Hermitian Majorana product by
introducing phase factors

\widehat{\gamma}_{S}=\widehat{\gamma}_{S}^{\dagger}=(-i)^{\absolutevalue{S}(\absolutevalue{S}-1)/2}\gamma_{S},

(430)

and they satisfy

\Tr(\widehat{\gamma}_S\widehat{\gamma}_{S'})=\delta_{S,S^{\prime}}\cdot 2^{n}.

(431)

Furthermore, there are
4^{n}
distinct Majorana products, so
\left\{\gamma_{S}\mid S\subseteq[2n]\right\}
and
\left\{\widehat{\gamma}_{S}\mid S\subseteq[2n]\right\}
are both orthogonal basis for
\mathcal{L}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes n})
.

The free unitaries
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{F}}
are the
n
-qubit free-fermionic (Gaussian) unitaries
\mathrm{M}_{n}
, which map Majorana operators to a linear combination of Majorana operators, in which the coefficients form an orthogonal matrix
R
. Each Gaussian unitary
U_{R}
is specified by
R
. Equivalently, in some literature
\mathrm{M}_{n}
is also referred to as those gates realizable by nearest-neighbor matchgate circuits
67
;
110
.

Definition 4

(Gaussian unitary)
.

For an orthogonal matrix
R\in\mathrm{O}(2n)
, a Gaussian unitary
U_{R}
is a unitary satisfying
\forall\mu\in[2n]
,

U_{R}^{\dagger}\gamma_{j}U_{R}=\sum_{k=1}^{2n}R_{j,k}\gamma_{k},

(432)

and the set of
n
-qubit Gaussian unitaries is
\mathrm{M}_{n}=\left\{U_{R}\mid R\in\mathrm{O}(2n)\right\}
.

An operator
A
is called parity-preserving if it commutes with the parity operator
Z^{\otimes n}
, i.e.,
[A,Z^{\otimes n}]=0
. Under the Majorana basis, this is equivalent to say that
A
is expanded by Majorana products
\gamma_{S}
with even degree
\absolutevalue{S}
only. Since parity is superselected, physical fermionic density matrices are parity-preserving, i.e. they commute with the parity operator. A pure parity-preserving state is therefore a parity eigenstate. We denote the set of valid fermionic states as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{F}

\displaystyle=\left\{\rho\in\mathcal{D}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes n})\middle\mid[\rho,Z^{\otimes n}]=0\right\}

(433)

\displaystyle=\left\{\rho\in\mathcal{D}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes n})\middle\mid\Tr(\gamma_S\rho)=0,\forall\absolutevalue{S}\textup{ is odd}\right\}.

Given an arbitrary density matrix
\rho\in\mathcal{D}
, we can certify that it is a valid fermionic state by measuring all odd-degree Majorana products (see Proposition
2
).

Proof of Proposition
2
.

Let
d=2^{n}
. According to Theorem 1 in Ref.
110
, the first- and second- order matchgate twirling formulas are:

1.

for operator
O_{1}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathbb{C}^{d}\right)
,

\Phi_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}^{(1)}(O_{1})=\underset{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{U^{\dagger}}O_{1}U\right]=\frac{1}{d}\Tr(O_1)\mathbb{I},

(434)

2.

for operator
O_{2}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\left(\mathbb{C}^{d}\right)^{\otimes 2}\right)
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}^{(2)}(O_{2})

\displaystyle=\underset{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{U^{\dagger}}^{\otimes 2}O_{2}U^{\otimes 2}\right]

(435)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{2n}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{1}\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{A_1}=k\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{2}\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{A_2}=k\end{subarray}}\binom{2n}{k}^{-1}\Tr(\left(\widehat{\gamma}_{A_2}\otimes\widehat{\gamma}_{A_2}\right)O_2)\widehat{\gamma}_{A_{1}}^{\otimes 2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{2n}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{1}\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{A_1}=k\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{2}\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{A_2}=k\end{subarray}}\binom{2n}{k}^{-1}\Tr(\left(\gamma_{A_2}^\dagger\otimes\gamma_{A_2}^\dagger\right)O_2)\gamma_{A_{1}}^{\otimes 2}.

So we have

\displaystyle\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\widehat{\gamma}}_{S},\mathrm{M}_{n},2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\underset{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(1-\Tr(U^\dagger\widehat{\gamma}_SU\rho)\right)^{2}\right]

(436)

\displaystyle=1-2\underset{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Tr(U^\dagger\widehat{\gamma}_SU\rho)\right]+\underset{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Tr({U^\dagger}^{\otimes 2}\widehat{\gamma}_S^{\otimes 2}U^{\otimes 2}\rho^{\otimes 2})\right]

\displaystyle=1-2\Tr(\Phi_{\mathrm{M}_n}^{(1)}\left(\widehat{\gamma}_S\right)\rho)+\Tr(\Phi_{\mathrm{M}_n}^{(2)}\left(\widehat{\gamma}_S^{\otimes 2}\right)\rho^{\otimes 2})

\displaystyle=1+\binom{2n}{\absolutevalue{S}}^{-1}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{T}=\absolutevalue{S}\end{subarray}}\Tr(\widehat{\gamma}_T\rho)^{2},

where we have used
\Tr(\widehat{\gamma}_S)=0
and

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}^{(2)}\left(\widehat{\gamma}_{S}^{\otimes 2}\right)=\underset{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{U^{\dagger}}^{\otimes 2}{\widehat{\gamma}_{S}}^{\otimes 2}U^{\otimes 2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{2n}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{T}=k\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{R}=k\end{subarray}}\binom{2n}{k}^{-1}{\widehat{\gamma}_{T}}^{\otimes 2}\Tr(\widehat{\gamma}_R\widehat{\gamma}_S)^{2}

(437)

\displaystyle=\binom{2n}{\absolutevalue{S}}^{-1}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{T}=\absolutevalue{S}\end{subarray}}{\widehat{\gamma}_{T}}^{\otimes 2}.

Notice that
\absolutevalue{S}
is odd, all physical fermionic states
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{F}
have

{\rm Tr}(\widehat{\gamma}_{T}\rho)=0
for
\absolutevalue{T}=\absolutevalue{S}
, including all fermionic Gaussian states.
For every parity-preserving fermionic state
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{F}
,

\norm{\rho}_{\widetilde{\widehat{\gamma}}_{S},\mathrm{M}_{n},2}=1
.
Conversely, checking this equality for all odd degrees
|S|=1,3,\dots,2n-1

is equivalent to the absence of odd Majorana components.
∎

F.2
Proof of Theorem
7
and Corollary
4

The set of
n
-qubit pure Gaussian (free-fermionic) states is those that can be generated from
\ket{0^n}
using a Gaussian unitary,
\mathcal{G}_{n}=\{U\ket{0^n}\mid U\in\mathrm{M}_{n}\}
. Then the set of free states is the convex hull of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
,
\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{G}_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{F}
, also known as the convex Gaussian states
88
.
It is known that for
n\leq 3
, all valid fermionic states are convex Gaussian
22
. Going beyond this, the four-qubit GHZ state

\ket{\textup{GHZ}_4}=\frac{\ket{0000}+\ket{1111}}{\sqrt{2}}

(438)

is a typical fermionic non-Gaussian state, which plays a role similar to the
T
-state in the magic resource theory that they can be used as an input to realize SWAP gate by a gadget, rendering matchgate circuits classically not efficiently simulable
54
;
87
.

We write

n=4m+r,\quad\textup{with }r\in\{0,1,2,3\}\textup{ and }m\geq 1

(439)

and define the states

\displaystyle\ket{\eta_0}

\displaystyle=\ket{\textup{GHZ}_4},

(440)

\displaystyle\ket{\eta_1}

\displaystyle=\ket{\textup{GHZ}_4}\otimes\ket{0}=\frac{\ket{00000}+\ket{11110}}{\sqrt{2}},

\displaystyle\ket{\eta_2}

\displaystyle=\ket{\textup{GHZ}_6},

\displaystyle\ket{\eta_3}

\displaystyle=\ket{\textup{GHZ}_6}\otimes\ket{0}=\frac{\ket{0000000}+\ket{1111110}}{\sqrt{2}}.

Set the projectors onto pure fermionic non-Gaussian states for
n=4m+r
,

P_{n}=\ket{\eta_r}\bra{\eta_r}\otimes(\ket{\mathrm{GHZ}_4}\bra{\mathrm{GHZ}_4})^{\otimes(m-1)}.

(441)

Consider the FLO (Gaussian) fidelity, defined as the maximum overlap between a pure state and any pure Gaussian state

F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\psi})=\max_{\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{G}_{n}}\absolutevalue{\langle\psi\mid\phi\rangle}^{2},

(442)

Lemma 1 in Ref.
93
shows that it is multiplicative for a fixed parity state vector
\ket{\psi}
and
\ket{\textup{GHZ}_4}
, i.e.,

F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\psi}\otimes\ket{\textup{GHZ}_4})=F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\psi})\times F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\textup{GHZ}_4}).

(443)

We then show the FLO fidelity of
n
-qubit GHZ states for even
n\geq 4
is
1/2
.

Lemma 7

(FLO fidelity of even-qubit GHZ states)
.

For even
n\geq 4
, the FLO fidelity of
n
-qubit GHZ state vector
\ket{\textup{GHZ}_n}
is
1/2
, i.e.,

F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\textup{GHZ}_n})=\max_{\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{G}_{n}}\absolutevalue{\langle\phi\mid\textup{GHZ}_n\rangle}^{2}=\frac{1}{2},

(444)

and the
n
-qubit vacuum state vector
\ket{0^n}
saturates the
bound.

Proof of Lemma
7
.

First notice that for
n
-qubit pure Gaussian state vector

\ket{\phi}
,

\absolutevalue{\langle\phi\mid\textup{GHZ}_n\rangle}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{\langle\phi\mid 0^n\rangle+\langle\phi\mid 1^n\rangle}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\absolutevalue{\langle\phi\mid 0^n\rangle}+\absolutevalue{\langle\phi\mid 1^n\rangle}\right)^{2}.

(445)

For
\ket{0^n}
, the only nonzero two-point Majorana expectations are

\langle 0^{n}|\gamma_{2l-1}\gamma_{2l}|0^{n}\rangle=i
.
For
|1^{n}\rangle
, since
Z_{l}|1^{n}\rangle=-|1^{n}\rangle
and

\gamma_{2l-1}\gamma_{2l}=iZ_{l}
, these expectations are
-i
,
while all off-pair expectations remain zero.
Denote the correlation matrices of
\ket{0^n}
and
\ket{1^n}
as
\Gamma_{0}
and
\Gamma_{1}
respectively, we have
\Gamma_{1}=-\Gamma_{0}
. Let
\Gamma_{\phi}
be the correlation matrix of
\ket{\phi}
, then the overlaps can be calculated as (see Lemma A7 in Ref.
14
and also Ref.
20
)

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\langle\phi\mid 0^n\rangle}^{2}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\mathrm{Pf}\left(\frac 12\left(\Gamma_\phi+\Gamma_0\right)\right)}

(446)

\displaystyle=\left(\det\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\Gamma_{\phi}+\Gamma_{0}\right)\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\det\left(\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{\phi}\left(I-\Gamma_{\phi}\Gamma_{0}\right)\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\det\left(\Gamma_{\phi}\right)\det\left(\frac{I-\Gamma_{\phi}\Gamma_{0}}{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\det\left(\frac{I-\Gamma_{\phi}\Gamma_{0}}{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where we have used the fact that the correlation matrix of a pure Gaussian state is antisymmetric and orthogonal, and that the determinant of an even-dimensional antisymmetric matrix equals the square of its Pfaffian. Similarly, we have

\absolutevalue{\langle\phi\mid 1^n\rangle}^{2}=\left(\det\left(\frac{I+\Gamma_{\phi}\Gamma_{0}}{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(447)

by replacing
\Gamma_{1}
with
-\Gamma_{0}
. Notice that
\Gamma_{\phi}\Gamma_{0}
is further a real orthogonal matrix, whose eigenvalues can be parametrized as

\Lambda(\Gamma_{\phi}\Gamma_{0})=\{e^{i\theta_{1}},e^{-i\theta_{1}},\cdots,e^{i\theta_{n}},e^{-i\theta_{n}}\},

(448)

with each
\theta_{j}\in[0,\pi)
. Then we have

\displaystyle\det\left(\frac{I-\Gamma_{\phi}\Gamma_{0}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{\lambda\in\Lambda(\Gamma_{\phi}\Gamma_{0})}\frac{1-\lambda}{2}

(449)

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1-e^{i\theta_{j}}}{2}\times\frac{1-e^{-i\theta_{j}}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1-\cos\theta_{j}}{2}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{2}\right),

and similarly

\det\left(\frac{I+\Gamma_{\phi}\Gamma_{0}}{2}\right)=\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{2}\right).

(450)

Inserting each term in Eq. (
445
), we thus obtain

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\langle\phi\mid\textup{GHZ}_n\rangle}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\absolutevalue{\langle\phi\mid 0^n\rangle}+\absolutevalue{\langle\phi\mid 1^n\rangle}\right)^{2}

(451)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\det\left(\frac{I-\Gamma_{\phi}\Gamma_{0}}{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}+\left(\det\left(\frac{I+\Gamma_{\phi}\Gamma_{0}}{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}\absolutevalue{\sin\left(\frac{\theta_j}2\right)}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}\absolutevalue{\cos\left(\frac{\theta_j}2\right)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}.

Since
n
is even, denote for each
l\in[n/2]

\displaystyle a_{l}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\sin\left(\frac{\theta_{2l-1}}2\right)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\absolutevalue{\sin\left(\frac{\theta_{2l}}2\right)}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(452)

\displaystyle b_{l}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\cos\left(\frac{\theta_{2l-1}}2\right)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\absolutevalue{\cos\left(\frac{\theta_{2l}}2\right)}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

It is worth noting that the absolute value operations in the above definitions could be safely removed, since

\sin\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{2}\right),\cos\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{2}\right)\geq 0,

(453)

for each parameter
\theta_{j}\in[0,\pi)
.
We first analyze the case when
n=4
, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\left(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{4}\absolutevalue{\sin\left(\frac{\theta_j}2\right)}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\prod\limits_{j=1}^{4}\absolutevalue{\cos\left(\frac{\theta_j}2\right)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=(a_{1}a_{2}+b_{1}b_{2})^{2}

(454)

\displaystyle\leq\left(a_{1}^{2}+b_{1}^{2}\right)\left(a_{2}^{2}+b_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\cos\left(\frac{\theta_{1}}{2}-\frac{\theta_{2}}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{\theta_{3}}{2}-\frac{\theta_{4}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 1,

and the equality is achieved when all
\theta_{j}
take the same value. Consider the following inequality:

\left(\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k}a_{l}+\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k}b_{l}\right)^{2}\leq\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k}\left(a_{l}^{2}+b_{l}^{2}\right).

(455)

We have proved it for
k=2
, assume this inequality holds for an integer
k
such that
2\leq k\leq n/2-1
, then

\displaystyle\left(\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k+1}a_{l}+\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k+1}b_{l}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k}a_{l}\right)\times a_{k+1}+\left(\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k}b_{l}\right)\times b_{k+1}\right)^{2}

(456)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left(\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k}a_{l}\right)^{2}+\left(\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k}b_{l}\right)^{2}\right)\left(a_{k+1}^{2}+b_{k+1}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k}\left(a_{l}^{2}+b_{l}^{2}\right)\right)\left(a_{k+1}^{2}+b_{k+1}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k+1}\left(a_{l}^{2}+b_{l}^{2}\right),

where we again use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. By induction, Eq. (
455
) holds for all
k\in\{2,\cdots,n/2\}
. Finally, we conclude

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\langle\phi\mid\textup{GHZ}_n\rangle}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}\absolutevalue{\sin\left(\frac{\theta_j}2\right)}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}\absolutevalue{\cos\left(\frac{\theta_j}2\right)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}

(457)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\prod\limits_{l=1}^{n/2}a_{l}+\prod\limits_{l=1}^{n/2}b_{l}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\prod\limits_{l=1}^{n/2}\left(a_{l}^{2}+b_{l}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\prod\limits_{l=1}^{n/2}\cos\left(\frac{\theta_{2l-1}}{2}-\frac{\theta_{2l}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}.

It is easy to check that
\ket{\phi}=\ket{0^n}
reaches the upper bound
1/2
.
∎

In particular, we have
F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\eta_0})=F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\eta_2})=1/2
. We then show for any
n
-qubit pure fixed-parity state vector
\ket{\psi}
,
F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\psi}\otimes\ket{0})=F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\psi})
. This can be seen from for any
(n+1)
-qubit pure Gaussian state vector
\ket{\phi}
,
\left(\mathbb{I}\otimes\bra{0}\right)\ket{\phi}
is an
n
-qubit pure Gaussian state. So

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\psi}\otimes\ket{0})

\displaystyle=\max_{\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{G}_{n+1}}\absolutevalue{\bra{\phi}(\ket{\psi}\otimes\ket{0})}^{2}

(458)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{G}_{n+1}}\absolutevalue{\bra{\phi}(\bI\otimes\ket{0})\ket{\psi}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\psi}).

On the other hand, let
\ket{\phi^*}=\arg\max_{\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{G}_{n}}\absolutevalue{\bra{\phi^*}\ket{\psi}}^{2}
, then
\ket{\phi^*}\otimes\ket{0}
is a
(n+1)
-qubit pure Gaussian state, thus

F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\psi}\otimes\ket{0})\geq\absolutevalue{\left(\bra{\phi^*}\otimes\bra{0}\right)\left(\ket{\psi}\otimes\ket{0}\right)}^{2}=F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\psi}).

(459)

So we have
F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\eta_1})=F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(\ket{\eta_3})=1/2
. Using Eq. (
443
) iteratively, we obtain

F_{\mathrm{FLO}}(P_{n})=\frac{1}{2^{\lfloor n/4\rfloor}},\qquad\forall n\geq 4.

(460)

By the convexity, we have for all
\sigma\in\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{G}_{n})
,

\Tr(\sigma P_n)\leq 2^{-\lfloor n/4\rfloor}.

(461)

Notice that the FLO fidelity of
P_{n}
decays exponentially with
n
, which indicates that
P_{n}
is a scalable and resource-sensitive operator as the seed operator to build a WE criterion for detecting fermionic non-Gaussianity. We then derive the explicit calculation for the WE
2,3
-criteria in Theorem
7
.

Proof of Theorem
7
.

We first introduce some notations. For each
k\in\{0,1,\dots,2n\}
let

\mathcal{H}_{k}=\operatorname{span}\{\gamma_{S}\mid\absolutevalue{S}=k\}
be the subspace spanned by degree-
k
Majorana products, and let
\Pi_{k}:\mathcal{L}\left(\left(\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)^{\otimes n}\right)\to\mathcal{H}_{k}
be the orthogonal projector onto
\mathcal{H}_{k}
. Denote
d=2^{n}
, define for operator
A\in\mathcal{L}\left(\left(\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)^{\otimes n}\right)
,

\displaystyle\Pi_{k}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\absolutevalue{S}=k}\Tr(\gamma_S^\dagger A)\gamma_{S}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\absolutevalue{S}=k}\Tr(\widehat{\gamma}_SA)\widehat{\gamma}_{S},

(462)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\subset[2n]\ \textup{disjoint}\\
\absolutevalue{A_i}=2\ell_{i}\end{subarray}}\Tr\left(A\gamma_{A_{1}}\gamma_{A_{2}}\right)\Tr\left(A\gamma_{A_{2}}\gamma_{A_{3}}\right)\Tr\left(A\gamma_{A_{3}}\gamma_{A_{1}}\right).

For a physical fermionic state
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{F}
, recall the even Majorana sector purities
B_{\ell}(\rho)
for
\ell\in\{0,1,\cdots,n\}
defined in Eq. (
75
) in the main text, we show

B_{\ell}(\rho)=\sum_{\absolutevalue{S}=2\ell}\Tr(\widehat{\gamma}_S\rho)^{2}=d\Tr\left(\Pi_{2\ell}(\rho)^{2}\right).

(463)

For any
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{F}
, we use Eq. (
431
), then

\displaystyle\Tr(\Pi_{2\ell}(\rho)^2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}S_{1}\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{S_1}=2\ell\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}S_{2}\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{S_2}=2\ell\end{subarray}}\Tr(\widehat{\gamma}_{S_1} \rho)\Tr(\widehat{\gamma}_{S_2} \rho)\Tr(\widehat{\gamma}_{S_1}\widehat{\gamma}_{S_2})

(464)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}S_{1}\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{S_1}=2\ell\end{subarray}}\Tr(\widehat{\gamma}_{S_1} \rho)^{2}=\frac{1}{d}B_{\ell}(\rho).

We now calculate the WE
2
-norm in Eq. (
90
).

\displaystyle\norm{\rho}_{P_{n},\mathrm{M}_{n},2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\underset{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Tr(U^\dagger P_n U\rho)^{2}\right]=\Tr\left[P_{n}^{\otimes{2}}\Phi_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}^{(2)}(\rho^{\otimes 2})\right]

(465)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{2n}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{1}\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{A_1}=k\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{2}\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{A_2}=k\end{subarray}}\binom{2n}{k}^{-1}\Tr(\gamma_{A_2}^\dagger P_n)^{2}\Tr(\gamma_{A_1}\rho)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\binom{2n}{2\ell}^{-1}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{2}\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{A_2}=2\ell\end{subarray}}\Tr(\gamma_{A_2}^\dagger P_n)^{2}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{1}\subseteq[2n]\\
\absolutevalue{A_1}=2\ell\end{subarray}}\Tr(\gamma_{A_1}^\dagger\rho)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\frac{\Tr\left(\Pi_{2\ell}(P_{n})^{2}\right)\Tr\left(\Pi_{2\ell}(\rho)^{2}\right)}{\binom{2n}{2\ell}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\frac{B_{\ell}(P_{n})B_{\ell}(\rho)}{\binom{2n}{2\ell}},

where in the second line we have used Eq. (
435
), in the third line we have used both
P_{n}
and
\rho
are parity-preserving and coefficients with odd-degree Majorana products vanish, and
\Tr(\gamma_{A_1}\rho)^{2}=\Tr(\gamma_{A_1}^\dagger\rho)^{2}
when
\absolutevalue{A_1}
is even.

For the WE
3
-norm, we recall the third-order matchgate twirling formula (see Theorem 1 in Ref.
110
): for operator
O_{3}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\left(\mathbb{C}^{d}\right)^{\otimes 3}\right)
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}^{(3)}(O_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\geq 0\\
k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}\leq 2n\end{subarray}}\binom{2n}{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},2n-k_{1}-k_{2}-k_{3}}^{-1}

(466)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\subset[2n]\ \textup{disjoint}\\
A^{\prime}_{1},A^{\prime}_{2},A^{\prime}_{3}\subset[2n]\ \textup{disjoint}\\
\absolutevalue{A_i}=\absolutevalue{A'_i}=k_{i}\end{subarray}}\Tr(\left((\gamma_{A_1}\gamma_{A_2})^\dagger\otimes(\gamma_{A_2}\gamma_{A_3})^\dagger\otimes(\gamma_{A_3}\gamma_{A_1})^\dagger\right)O_3)\gamma_{A_{1}^{\prime}}\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{2}}\otimes\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{2}}\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{3}}\otimes\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{3}}\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{1}}.

Then we have

\norm{\rho}_{P_{n},\mathrm{M}_{n},3}^{3}=\underset{U\sim\mu_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Tr(U^\dagger P_n U\rho)^{3}\right]=\Tr\left[P_{n}^{\otimes{3}}\Phi_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}^{(3)}(\rho^{\otimes 3})\right]

(467)

Inserting
O_{3}=\rho^{\otimes 3}
, since
\rho
is parity-preserving, the terms in
\Phi_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}^{(3)}(\rho^{\otimes 3})
vanish whenever one of
k_{1}+k_{2}
,
k_{2}+k_{3}
, and
k_{3}+k_{1}
is odd. Equivalently, a nonzero contribution from
\rho
requires
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}
to have the same parity. However, we show that the all-odd case vanishes when we consider terms related to
P_{n}
.

Define the Majorana support of the seed projector
P_{n}
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(P_{n})=\left\{S\subseteq[2n]\middle\mid\Tr(P_n\gamma_S)\neq 0\right\}.

(468)

Since
P_{n}
is parity-preserving, we have

\displaystyle S\in\mathsf{S}(P_{n})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\absolutevalue{S}\equiv 0\pmod{2}.

(469)

The seed
P_{n}
is a tensor product of GHZ-type stabilizer projectors and computational basis stabilizer projectors. Hence,
P_{n}
is a pure stabilizer projector. Therefore, if
S\in\mathsf{S}(P_{n})
, then the Majorana monomial
\gamma_{S}
, up to its scalar phase, corresponds to an element of the abelian stabilizer group of
P_{n}
.
For arbitrary subsets
S,T\subseteq[2n]
, the Majorana commutation rule gives

\displaystyle\gamma_{S}\gamma_{T}=(-1)^{\absolutevalue{S}\absolutevalue{T}-\absolutevalue{S\cap T}}\gamma_{T}\gamma_{S}.

(470)

If
S,T\in\mathsf{S}(P_{n})
, then the corresponding stabilizer elements commute. Since
\absolutevalue{S}
and
\absolutevalue{T}
are even by Eq. (
469
), Eq. (
470
) implies

\displaystyle S,T\in\mathsf{S}(P_{n})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\absolutevalue{S\cap T}\equiv 0\pmod{2}.

(471)

Now consider the contraction with
P_{n}^{\otimes 3}
of one output term in Eq. (
466
):

\Tr\left[P_{n}^{\otimes 3}\left(\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{1}}\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{2}}\otimes\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{2}}\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{3}}\otimes\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{3}}\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{1}}\right)\right]=\Tr(P_n\gamma_{A'_1}\gamma_{A'_2})\Tr(P_n\gamma_{A'_2}\gamma_{A'_3})\Tr(P_n\gamma_{A'_3}\gamma_{A'_1}).

(472)

Suppose that this product is nonzero. Since
A^{\prime}_{1},A^{\prime}_{2},A^{\prime}_{3}
are pairwise disjoint, for each
i\neq j
we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{i}}\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{j}}=\omega_{i,j}\gamma_{A^{\prime}_{i}\cup A^{\prime}_{j}},\qquad\omega_{i,j}\in\{+1,-1\}.

(473)

Therefore the nonzero condition in Eq. (
472
) implies

\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{1}\cup A^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathsf{S}(P_{n}),\qquad A^{\prime}_{2}\cup A^{\prime}_{3}\in\mathsf{S}(P_{n}),\qquad A^{\prime}_{3}\cup A^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathsf{S}(P_{n}).

(474)

Applying Eq. (
471
) to the first and third sets in Eq. (
474
), and using the pairwise disjointness of
A^{\prime}_{1},A^{\prime}_{2},A^{\prime}_{3}
, gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\absolutevalue{(A'_1\cup A'_2)\cap(A'_3\cup A'_1)}=\absolutevalue{A'_1}\pmod{2},

(475)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\absolutevalue{(A'_1\cup A'_2)\cap(A'_2\cup A'_3)}=\absolutevalue{A'_2}\pmod{2},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\absolutevalue{(A'_2\cup A'_3)\cap(A'_3\cup A'_1)}=\absolutevalue{A'_3}\pmod{2}.

Thus every output term surviving the contraction with
P_{n}^{\otimes 3}
must satisfy

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{A'_1}\equiv\absolutevalue{A'_2}\equiv\absolutevalue{A'_3}\equiv 0\pmod{2}.

(476)

Since
\absolutevalue{A'_i}=k_{i}
, this means that all terms with at least one odd
k_{i}
vanish after contraction with
P_{n}^{\otimes 3}
. Combining this with the parity-preserving condition on
\rho
, the only remaining sectors are the all-even sectors. Therefore we set

\displaystyle k_{i}=2\ell_{i},\qquad i=1,2,3.

(477)

So the possible non-vanishing terms are those with all even
k_{i}=2\ell_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\Tr(\left((\gamma_{A_1}\gamma_{A_2})^\dagger\otimes(\gamma_{A_2}\gamma_{A_3})^\dagger\otimes(\gamma_{A_3}\gamma_{A_1})^\dagger\right)\rho^{\otimes 3})

(478)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Tr((\gamma_{A_1}\gamma_{A_2})^\dagger\rho)\Tr((\gamma_{A_2}\gamma_{A_3})^\dagger\rho)\Tr((\gamma_{A_3}\gamma_{A_1})^\dagger\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\Tr(\rho\gamma_{A_1}\gamma_{A_2})(-1)^{\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}\Tr(\rho\gamma_{A_2}\gamma_{A_3})(-1)^{\ell_{3}+\ell_{1}}\Tr(\rho\gamma_{A_3}\gamma_{A_1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Tr(\rho\gamma_{A_1}\gamma_{A_2})\Tr(\rho\gamma_{A_2}\gamma_{A_3})\Tr(\rho\gamma_{A_3}\gamma_{A_1}).

Using the definition in Eq. (
462
) and the above equation, the WE
3
-norm is

\displaystyle\norm{\rho}_{P_{n},\mathrm{M}_{n},3}^{3}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[P_{n}^{\otimes{3}}\Phi_{\mathrm{M}_{n}}^{(3)}(\rho^{\otimes 3})\right]

(479)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d^{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\geq 0\\
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}\leq n\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(P_{n})\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\rho)}{\binom{2n}{2\ell_{1},2\ell_{2},2\ell_{3},2n-2(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})}},

which proves Eq. (
90
).
The threshold values for convex Gaussian states can be evaluated at an arbitrary pure Gaussian state. We use
\ket{0^n}
and calculate the functions

\displaystyle B_{\ell}(\ket{0^n}\bra{0^n})

\displaystyle=d\Tr\left(\Pi_{2\ell}(\ket{0^n}\bra{0^n})^{2}\right)=\binom{n}{\ell},

(480)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(\ket{0^n}\bra{0^n})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}\binom{n}{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},n-(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})}.

Inserting Eq. (
480
) to Eq. (
465
) and Eq. (
479
), we prove Eq. (
91
) in the main text.

Short proof of Eq. (
480
): We rewrite
\outerproduct{0^n}{0^n}=d^{-1}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left(\mathbb{I}-i\gamma_{2j-1}\gamma_{2j}\right)
.
The contribution to degree-
2\ell
Majorana sector of
\ket{0^n}\bra{0^n}
is obtained by choosing
\ell
of the
n
commuting pair monomials
-i\gamma_{2j-1}\gamma_{2j}
from the product expansion. There are
\binom{n}{\ell}
such terms, each with coefficient
d^{-1}
and orthogonal to all others, which proves the first equation in Eq. (
480
).

For the second equation, denote the index of neighboring Majorana pairs as
p_{j}=\{2j-1,2j\}
. Each of the three traces in
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}
(cf. Eq. (
462
)) is nonzero if and only if
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
,
A_{2}\cup A_{3}
, and
A_{3}\cup A_{1}
are all union of disjoint pairs
\{p_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
. Notice that
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
are disjoint sets, that is to say, for each
j\in[n]
, either
\{2j-1,2j\}\subseteq A_{i}
for some
i
, or
2j-1,2j\notin A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}
. The number of such choices is the multinomial coefficient
\binom{n}{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},n-(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})}
, i.e.,
\ell_{i}
pairs are assigned to
A_{i}
and the remaining
n-(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})
pairs are left unassigned. Each assignment contributes

(-i)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\times(-i)^{\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}\times(-i)^{\ell_{3}+\ell_{1}}=(-1)^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}

(481)

to the summation, which proves the second equation in Eq. (
480
).
∎

To obtain concrete WE
2,3
-criteria, we still need to evaluate the functions
B_{\ell}(P_{n})
and
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(P_{n})
. They are a bit tedious to calculate, and we provide the generating functions in the following sequential lemmas: Lemma
8
for
n=4
, Lemma
9
for
n=4m
, Lemma
10
for
5\leq n\leq 7
, and Lemma
11
for all
n
.

We first look into the 4-qubit GHZ state
P_{4}
.

Lemma 8

(Function for GHZ
4
)
.

For the
4
-qubit
GHZ
state
P_{4}=\ket{\textup{GHZ}_4}\bra{\textup{GHZ}_4}
, the values of functions defined in Eq. (
462
) and Eq. (
463
) are given by their generating functions

\displaystyle P[z]\coloneqq\sum_{\ell=0}^{4}B_{\ell}(P_{4})z^{\ell}

\displaystyle=1+14z^{2}+z^{4},

(482)

\displaystyle T[x,y,z]\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\geq 0\\
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}\leq 4\end{subarray}}\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(P_{4})x^{\ell_{1}}y^{\ell_{2}}z^{\ell_{3}}

\displaystyle=1+14\left(x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}+x^{2}y^{2}+y^{2}z^{2}+z^{2}x^{2}\right)+x^{4}+y^{4}+z^{4}-168xyz.

Proof of Lemma
8
.

Rewrite
P_{4}
in terms of Majorana products as

\displaystyle P_{4}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{16}\sum_{S\subseteq[8]}\varepsilon(S)\gamma_{S},\qquad\textup{with }\varepsilon(S)\in\{0,\pm 1\},

(483)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{16}\Bigl(\gamma_{\emptyset}-\gamma_{\{1,2,3,4\}}-\gamma_{\{1,2,5,6\}}-\gamma_{\{1,2,7,8\}}-\gamma_{\{3,4,5,6\}}-\gamma_{\{3,4,7,8\}}-\gamma_{\{5,6,7,8\}}+\gamma_{\{1,3,5,7\}}

\displaystyle-\gamma_{\{1,3,6,8\}}-\gamma_{\{1,4,5,8\}}-\gamma_{\{1,4,6,7\}}-\gamma_{\{2,3,5,8\}}-\gamma_{\{2,3,6,7\}}-\gamma_{\{2,4,5,7\}}+\gamma_{\{2,4,6,8\}}

\displaystyle+\gamma_{\{1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8\}}\Bigr).

A direct calculation leads to
B_{\ell}(P_{4})=\absolutevalue{\{S\subseteq[8]\mid\abs{S}=2\ell,\varepsilon(S)\neq 0\}}
and the first line in Eq. (
482
).

For
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}
, notice that if
A,B\subseteq[8]
are disjoint, then

\gamma_{A}\gamma_{B}=(-1)^{\nu(A,B)}\gamma_{A\cup B},\qquad\textup{with }\nu(A,B)=\absolutevalue{\{(a,b)\in A\times B\mid a>b\}}.

(484)

Hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(P_{4})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\subseteq[8]\ \mathrm{disjoint}\\
\absolutevalue{A_i}=2\ell_{i}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle(-1)^{\nu(A_{1},A_{2})+\nu(A_{2},A_{3})+\nu(A_{3},A_{1})}\times\varepsilon(A_{1}\cup A_{2})\varepsilon(A_{2}\cup A_{3})\varepsilon(A_{3}\cup A_{1}).

(485)

Because
\varepsilon(S)\neq 0
only for
\absolutevalue{S}=0,4,8
(cf. Eq. (
483
)), a necessary condition for a nonzero contribution is

\absolutevalue{A_i\cup A_j}=2(\ell_{i}+\ell_{j})\in\{0,4,8\},

(486)

i.e.

\ell_{i}+\ell_{j}\in\{0,2,4\}\qquad(1\leq i<j\leq 3).

(487)

With
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\geq 0
and
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}\leq 4
, the only possibilities are

(0,0,0),\quad(2,0,0)\textup{ and permutations},\quad(2,2,0)\textup{ and permutations},\quad(4,0,0)\textup{ and permutations},\quad(1,1,1).

(488)

The first four cases are listed here:

1.

\mathcal{T}_{0,0,0}(P_{4})=1
.

2.

If
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=(2,0,0)
, then
A_{2}=A_{3}=\emptyset
and

\mathcal{T}_{2,0,0}(P_{4})=\sum_{|A_{1}|=4}\varepsilon(A_{1})^{2}=14.

(489)

By symmetry, the same holds for the coefficients of
y^{2}
and
z^{2}
.

3.

If
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=(4,0,0)
, then
A_{1}=[8]
and
A_{2}=A_{3}=\emptyset
, so

\mathcal{T}_{4,0,0}(P_{4})=\varepsilon([8])^{2}=1.

(490)

By symmetry, the same holds for
y^{4}
and
z^{4}
.

4.

If
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=(2,2,0)
, then
A_{3}=\emptyset
and
A_{2}=[8]\setminus A_{1}
. In this case
\nu(A_{1},A_{2})\equiv\sum_{a\in A_{1}}a\pmod{2}
,
\nu(A_{2},A_{3})=\nu(A_{3},A_{1})=0
.
The complement of every degree-
4
support in Eq. (
483
) is again a degree-
4
support, and for every such ordered pair

(-1)^{\nu(A_{1},A_{2})+\nu(A_{2},\emptyset)+\nu(\emptyset,A_{1})}\varepsilon(A_{1}\cup A_{2})\varepsilon(A_{2})\varepsilon(A_{1})=1.

(491)

Hence

\mathcal{T}_{2,2,0}(P_{4})=14.

(492)

By symmetry, the same holds for the coefficients of
x^{2}z^{2}
and
y^{2}z^{2}
.

It remains to compute
\mathcal{T}_{1,1,1}(P_{4})
. In this case, each
A_{i}
has size
2
. A direct inspection of the
14
degree-
4
supports shows that the nonzero contributions come from three types of unordered decompositions of six indices into three disjoint
2
-subsets:

•

Type I:
the omitted two-set is one neighboring pair. There are
\binom{4}{1}=4
such cases, one example is

[8]\setminus\{7,8\}=\{1,2\}\sqcup\{3,4\}\sqcup\{5,6\}.

(493)

For the ordered representative

(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})=(\{1,2\},\{3,4\},\{5,6\}),

(494)

we have

\varepsilon(\{1,2,3,4\})=\varepsilon(\{3,4,5,6\})=\varepsilon(\{1,2,5,6\})=-1,

(495)

and

(-1)^{\nu(\{1,2\},\{3,4\})+\nu(\{3,4\},\{5,6\})+\nu(\{5,6\},\{1,2\})}=1,

(496)

so the contribution is
-1
. Since each unordered decomposition gives
3!=6
ordered triples, this example contributes
-6
. After checking, all examples in Type I contribute
-6
, so overall Type I contributes
-24
.

•

Type II:
the omitted two-set uses one index from each of two different neighboring pairs, and both have the same parity. There are
\binom{4}{2}\times 2=12
such cases, one example is

[8]\setminus\{6,8\}=\{1,3\}\sqcup\{2,4\}\sqcup\{5,7\}.

(497)

For the ordered representative

(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})=(\{1,3\},\{2,4\},\{5,7\}),

(498)

we have

\varepsilon(\{1,2,3,4\})=-1,\qquad\varepsilon(\{2,4,5,7\})=-1,\qquad\varepsilon(\{1,3,5,7\})=1,

(499)

and

(-1)^{\nu(\{1,3\},\{2,4\})+\nu(\{2,4\},\{5,7\})+\nu(\{5,7\},\{1,3\})}=(-1)^{1+0+0}=-1.

(500)

Thus this representative also contributes
-1
. Each unordered decomposition gives
3!=6
ordered triples, so this example contributes
-6
. After checking, all examples in Type II contribute
-6
, so overall Type II contributes
-72
.

•

Type III:
the omitted two-set uses one index from each of two different neighboring pairs, and they have different parities. There are
\binom{4}{2}\times 2=12
such cases, one example is

[8]\setminus\{6,7\}=\{1,4\}\sqcup\{2,3\}\sqcup\{5,8\}.

(501)

For the ordered representative

(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})=(\{1,4\},\{2,3\},\{5,8\}),

(502)

we have

\varepsilon(\{1,2,3,4\})=-1,\qquad\varepsilon(\{1,4,5,8\})=-1,\qquad\varepsilon(\{2,3,5,8\})=-1,

(503)

and

(-1)^{\nu(\{1,4\},\{2,3\})+\nu(\{2,3\},\{5,8\})+\nu(\{5,8\},\{1,4\})}=(-1)^{2+0+4}=1.

(504)

Thus this representative also contributes
-1
. Each unordered decomposition gives
3!=6
ordered triples, so this example contributes
-6
. After checking, all examples in Type III contribute
-6
, so overall Type III contributes
-72
.

Therefore

\mathcal{T}_{1,1,1}(P_{4})=-24-72-72=-168.

(505)

Combining all the above calculations recovers the second line in Eq. (
482
).
∎

Based on Lemma
8
, the evaluation for
P_{n}
with
n=4m
is provided as below.

Lemma 9

(Function for
P_{4m}
)
.

For the
m
-fold tensor product of the 4-qubit GHZ state
P_{4m}=P_{4}^{\otimes m}
, the values of functions defined in Eq. (
462
) and Eq. (
463
) are given by

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}B_{\ell}(P_{4m})z^{\ell}

\displaystyle=P[z]^{m},

(506)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\geq 0\\
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}\leq n\end{subarray}}\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(P_{4m})x^{\ell_{1}}y^{\ell_{2}}z^{\ell_{3}}

\displaystyle=T[x,y,z]^{m},

where
P[z]
and
T[x,y,z]
are defined in Eq. (
482
).

Proof of Lemma
9
.

We first decompose the index set, the Hilbert space, and the
m
-fold tensor product of the 4-qubit GHZ state as

\displaystyle=I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{m},\quad\textup{with }I_{j}=\{8(j-1)+1,\cdots,8j\},

(507)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{4m}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}_{4}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{4}^{(m)},

\displaystyle P_{4m}

\displaystyle=P_{4}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes P_{4}^{(m)},\quad\textup{with }P_{4}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}^{(j)}).

Notice that
P_{4}\in\mathcal{D}_{F}
consists of only even-degree Majorana operators by Eq. (
483
), we have for each
j\in[m]
(cf. Eq. (
462
)),

P_{4}^{(j)}=\sum_{i_{j}=0}^{4}\Pi_{2i_{j}}\left(P_{4}^{(j)}\right),

(508)

so

P_{4m}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{i_{j}=0}^{4}\Pi_{2i_{j}}\left(P_{4}^{(j)}\right)\right)=\sum_{i_{j}=0}^{4}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\Pi_{2i_{j}}\left(P_{4}^{(j)}\right),

(509)

and its projection onto subspace
\mathcal{H}_{2\ell}
is

\Pi_{2\ell}\left(P_{4m}\right)=\sum_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{m}=\ell}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\Pi_{2i_{j}}\left(P_{4}^{(j)}\right).

(510)

Then we have

\Tr(\Pi_{2\ell}\left(P_{4m}\right)^2)=\sum_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{m}=\ell}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Tr(\Pi_{2i_j}\left(P_4^{(j)}\right)^2),

(511)

since
\Tr\left(\Pi_{2i_{j}}\left(P_{4}^{(j)}\right)\Pi_{2i_{j}}\left(P_{4}^{(j)}\right)\right)=0
if
i_{j}\neq i^{\prime}_{j}
.
The generating function in the first line of Eq. (
506
) is

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{4m}B_{\ell}(P_{4m})z^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{4m}2^{4m}\Tr\left(\Pi_{2\ell}(P_{4m})^{2}\right)z^{\ell}

(512)

\displaystyle=2^{4m}\sum_{\ell=0}^{4m}\sum_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{m}=\ell}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Tr(\Pi_{2i_j}\left(P_4^{(j)}\right)^2)\right)z^{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{m}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{4}2^{4}\Tr(\Pi_{2i}\left(P_4\right)^2)z^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{4}B_{\ell}(P_{4})z^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=P[z]^{m}

Consider the sets
\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}
appearing in the summation in Eq. (
462
), under the decomposition in Eq. (
507
), we define
A_{i}^{(j)}=\{a-8(j-1)\mid a\in A_{i}\cap I_{j}\}\subseteq[8]
, for
i\in[3]
and
j\in[m]
. Denote the projection onto
\mathcal{H}^{(j)}
as
\Pi^{(j)}
, then we have for
i\in[3]
,

\gamma_{A_{i}}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\Pi^{(j)}\left(\gamma_{A_{i}}\right)=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\gamma_{A_{i}^{(j)}}

(513)

since each
\gamma_{A_{i}}
is a tensor product of single-qubit Paulis. Thus

\Tr(P_n\gamma_{A_1}\gamma_{A_2})=\Tr(\bigotimes_{j=1}^mP_4^{(j)}\bigotimes_{j=1}^m\gamma_{A_1^{(j)}}\bigotimes_{j=1}^m\gamma_{A_2^{(j)}})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Tr(P_4\gamma_{A_1^{(j)}}\gamma_{A_2^{(j)}}),

(514)

similar equations hold for
\Tr(P_n\gamma_{A_2}\gamma_{A_3})
and
\Tr(P_n\gamma_{A_3}\gamma_{A_1})
. Notice that
P_{4}
consists of only Majorana operators with degree
\{0,4,8\}
by Eq. (
483
), the product
\Tr(P_n\gamma_{A_1}\gamma_{A_2})\Tr(P_n\gamma_{A_2}\gamma_{A_3})\Tr(P_n\gamma_{A_3}\gamma_{A_1})
is nonzero if and only if for each
j\in[m]
,

\absolutevalue{A_1^{(j)}}+\absolutevalue{A_2^{(j)}},\quad\absolutevalue{A_2^{(j)}}+\absolutevalue{A_3^{(j)}},\quad\absolutevalue{A_3^{(j)}}+\absolutevalue{A_1^{(j)}}

(515)

all take values in
\{0,4,8\}
, which implies each
\absolutevalue{A_i^{(j)}}\eqqcolon 2\ell_{i}^{(j)}
is even. Since
A_{i}^{(j)}
are all disjoint subsets of
[8]
,
\ell_{1}^{(j)},\ell_{2}^{(j)},\ell_{3}^{(j)}\geq 0
and
\ell_{1}^{(j)}+\ell_{2}^{(j)}+\ell_{3}^{(j)}\leq 4
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}}(P_{4m})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\subset[8m]\ \textup{disjoint}\\
\absolutevalue{A_i}=2\ell_{i}\end{subarray}}\Tr\left(P_{4m}\gamma_{A_{1}}\gamma_{A_{2}}\right)\Tr\left(P_{4m}\gamma_{A_{2}}\gamma_{A_{3}}\right)\Tr\left(P_{4m}\gamma_{A_{3}}\gamma_{A_{1}}\right)

(516)
